CiOUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON.— The STREET IMPROVEMENT COMMITTEE of the COUNCIL of the COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON invite APPQCATIONS for the position of BUILDING INSPECTOR in their Borough Engineer and Surveyor's Office. be must be able to prepare plans for architectural work, with experience in taking out quantities, if required, and with a knowledge of building Bye-laws and Publio Health Acts and the preparation of plans and reports of insanitary property as required by the Medical Officer of Health. Salary .£125 per annum. Applications in candidate's own handwriting, stating age, experence, preferably in Municipal work, and enclosing copies of not more than three recent testi- monials, to be sent to my office, endorsed" Building Inspector." before 12 o'clock noon on Thursday, the 4th day of July. Canvassing the members of Committees or Council either directly or indirectly will be held a disqualification. Copies of duties can be obtained on application to the Borough Engineer and Survever. THOMAS LONODIN, Borough Engineer and Surveyor. Borough Engineer and Surveyor's Office, June 14th, 1912.

WYKN]

From the "Feathered World."

These are ene of the most popular varieties of fowls of the present day. For a long time past now they have pressed the ( )rpingtons for pride of place. The renewed popularity of the White Orpington of about three years ago has helped very much to stay the onward march of the White Wyandotte. Neverthe- less, the White Dottes, are still very popular and deservedly so, for they are a most useful breed of fowls. They are more or less an all-round variety. Their chief forte however is egg production and as winter layers they might have an equal, but they certainly cannot be surpassed. When crossed with the Ffoudan the progeny from this mating make very fine layers indeed.

_FOR EXHIBITION ITRI'ISEs.

The White Wyandotte is not an amateurs fowl so far as exhibiting goes. For one reason they are a white fowl and need great care in preparing for shows. Then again, the competition in this variety is very keen. You would run against something 'very hot' in the show pen; and unless you were both fortunate and clever you would stand very little chance of success. Those who do fancy these fowls for show purposes would do well to get the best only. Other- wise it would mean a waste of energy and money as well.

NOTES ron JUNE.

During this month the poultry keeper should be in the thick of his work. Attending to broodies and the last batch of chicks, keeping the young stock going and getting rid of the old stuck —all this means work ! Work ! ! Work !! ! The secret of success in poultry farming is work. Get to your stock in the early morning. Let the youngsters get out into the grass so that they may find the 'early worm.' Open wide the abed doors so that the sun and air may purify the sleeping quarters. All drinking and feeding utensils should be thoroughly washed clean and daily rinsed with Condy's Fluid. C IRE FOR rill: YOUNG STOCK. It is highly necessary at this period of the year to carefully examine all young stock. If any are infested with verminNive them a good dusting with insect / powder. I cannot reiterate the importance of this part of the work too much. Neglected chickens are soon stunted in growth both in body and feather, and it pays to look after them well in this direction. Do not give the young cockerels too much liberty ; small pens are the best because they fatten much quicker and are thus soon cif your hands. Turn them into money so that you are supplied with revenue for the better advancement of the female youngsters. Do not force the early pullets. Let them have proper time to mature in the body, and as a result they will give a better return in eggs when they commence to lay. Rigidly avoid overcrowding. Add a little Epsom salts to the drinking water once a week. This is a great purifier cf the blood and it helps very materially to tone up the whole system. Keep the drinking water pure and never let it stand in the hot sun. As i have frequently stated ; more deaths are caused through impure water than any other cause. Don't leave food lying about. As soon as the stock have had sufficient take away any that is left over. _ QUERIES.

I have quite a number of queries td hand this week. It seems a pity that readers do not make more use of this column than they, have hitherto done. Don't be shy. The column is yours, and why not make use of it?

_PALE YOLKS.

One reader complains of the yolks being pale. Perhaps it is the cause of your feeding? Give your fowls plenty of green food a4d add a few handsful of Indian corn to the grain occasionally and you will soon get better results.

ACCOM3IODAtION.

Constant Reader.—l have a run measuring 10 yards long by 8 yards wide. How many fowls will this accommodate? And what would be the best breed to keep for egg-production ? Answer. —I would not advise you to keep more than eight hens an a run of those dimensions. The best variety for egg production in so small a run can be selected (according to your special fancy) from any of the following varieties: White Leghorns. Black Leg- horns,Blue Andalusians, Black Ilin,,rcas or Anconas, if you want a mixture in the colour of the eggs? Then I should advisq you to keep four Croad Langshan hens and four of any from the foregoing list.

BLoOI, SPOT&

Anxious Moments.— Complains of finding specks of blood in the eggs. Ibis is caused through the rupture of a small blood-vessel in the ovary, generally the result of over stimulation.

Answer —Cease giving any animal-food for a week or two. Add a little EFsom salts to the drinking water, they will soon be alright again. When you resume giving animal-food, see that it is chopped up very fine. When given in large pieces some of the fowls are apt to get more than their share and the above result follows. It is a good plan to purchase a mincing machine for this purpose. And when ones stock is not a large one it is not much trouble to mince the meat. Of course where a largo stock are kept it is more advisable to purchase a green bone mill.

THE RD ITIF,II NEW-LAID EGG. A (HEAT INDUSTRY IN THE MAKING.

We have ample evidence before us to prove without doubt that the supply of the British new-laid egg is increasing by leaps and hounds. The British hen at the present time is a highly respected creature. She must now be scientifically fed and housed, receiving every care on the part of poultry-keepers. That section of the Poultry World devoting its attention to the production of the new-laid egg is rapidly swelling in numbers, ard yet the supply does not nearly equal the demand. Notwithstanding the sale of the Foreign egg in our markets, the British egg will always fetch the best prices.

MARKETING TUE BRITISH EGI. 'Whilst the British new-laid egg trade is making large strides, our utility poultry-keepers may yet learn a lesson from the foreigner. The latter pays every attention to cleanliness, colour, and uniformity in size to make his egg wares high class, and in these respects we must not allow ourselves to be beaten. Old-fashioned methods must be forsaken. In many cases the British producer is apt to send to market brown, white, small, clean and soiled eggs together in one consignment, which speaks for nothing but ignorant methods. It is therefore the duty cf,every producer to send his eggs to the market properly packed and graded, and in this way it will be impos eible for anyone even to say that the British new-laid egg does not pay to produce.—(Prom " Poultry World.")

KING RECEIVES MR ASQUITH.

Mr. Arquith returned to London on Monday morning from his Meditorraneauftrip. He called at Buckingham Palace and was received in audience by the King. The interview lasted about half an hour.

FOOTBALL NEWS.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

Members to Pay for Cup-Ties.

The annual meeting of the Northern Rugby Union was held on Tuesday night at the George Hotel,liudders- field, Mr George Taylor, Wigan, presiding. The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the annual report and balance sheet, which has already been published, said they had had a loss of over £2OO, but when it came to be analysed it would be seen they had been through a very exceptional season, and it WAS quite possible to trace where the loss had been. In the Test Matches with the Australasians they had met with heavy losses, as also was the case with the exhibition match at Plymouth. The losses at Nottingham and Bristol should be taken into consideration by the committee, and in future in allocating representative matches the com- mittee wguld sei iously have to consider the question of granting representative matches to some quarters where it was likely the results would, if not yielding profit, not involve a loss. Such games should be tried in some of the Northern Union centres.

Mr, A. J. Boynton (Hull) seconded, and said he was of an opinion that if the representative matches had teen distributed over the Northern Union area, results would have been better.

Mr. Moss (Bramlev) objected to the granting of money to the Ebbw Vale and Coventry clubs. It was that sort of thing, he said, which kept his club in such a lowly position. The Chairman said the money to Coventry and Ebbw Vale were not pants, bat for expenses in coming North to fulfil engagements. They did not want to do with- out those clubs and they must encourage them to some extent.

An interesting discussion followed, on the considera- tion of the financial statement of the British Playing Fields Society. Mr. Hardwick (Leeds) suggested that something should be done in regard to the disposal of interest on accumulated money, and if they studied the interests of the union they would spend such money in encouraging the development of junior football throughout the Northern Union area. The Leeds and Hunslet clubs were going to spend £lOO per annnm on intermediates, and juniors, and the Northern Union should give clubs a lead in that respect. Mr Platt (Oldham), said it was all right being generous, but they must have soundness. The present system was working well, by assisting clubs to assist themselves by producing good players. He had under- taken to raise £250 in his district. The Northern Union Committee would do justice to the fund and the junior clubs if they were trusted. The balance-sheet was adopted. Mr. J. B. Cooke moved, on behalf of the Union Corn. mittee,the following amendment to professional Law 1, Sub-section A, by adding at end, " no player shall be considered a professional unless be receives a fixed amount per match." Mr. Wood said they had the Workmen's Compensa- tion Act in force, and every player had been declared to be a workmao within the meaning of that Act. If he were registered as a professional, and received wages for playing football, it was only right he should come under that category, but where a player received only a small sum which, under the rules of the Union made him a professional, it would be a hardship on him if he were claimed to be a workman under the Compensation Act. A Wigan League representative seconded, and the motion was carried. In respect of the amendment proposed to Challenge Cop, Rule 8, moved by Mr. Lyon. of Hull, the !nover said if they made everyone pay to go to cup-ties it would be better for clubs. Mr. Mercer (St. Helens) reconded. Mr. Ross (Bramley) thought the wealthy clubs should give way and help poorer visiting clubs. Mr Woods (Leeds) opposed oil behalf of guinea members ; Mr, J Goldthorpe (Leeds) also opposed on similar grounds. Mr. J. A. Boynton (Hull) supported the Union pro- posal. He said that Normanton and Hull had slaareclin £175, whereas in a later tie, against a richer club, with a gate of over 20,000, they had only shared £159. Mr. Platt (Oldham) opposed the proposal on behalf of the Oldham Club, and Mr. J. Clifford (Huddersfield) also opposed. • The voting resulted : For, 23 ; against, 18. The motion, only requiring a bare majority, was therefore. carried.

NORTHERN UNION LEAGUE MEETING.

At a meeting of the Committee of the Northern Union League, which preceded the annual meeting of The League, at Huddersfield, on Wednesday evening, the Runcorn Club were ordered to pay .£fi 55. to Wigan Hisbfleld Club in respect of a broken engagement. Mr J B Cooke (Wakefield Trinity) presided at the annual meeting of the League. Runcorn, Bradford, and Bromley, the three clubs at the bottom of the League table, were again re-elected. The alteration of rules was then considered, Oldham Club proposed that during the season when the kick-off was 2.45 it should in future be 2.30. The alteration was proposed in order to prevent the possibility of all games being adandoned, Hull Kingston Rovers, Wigan, and Swinton opposed the alteration, the Lancashire clubs' representative arguing that colliers would be unable to get to the matches in time. An addition proposed by the League Committee making it a penalty for a player to approach a club, as it is at present a penalty for a club to approach a player, was carried on the ground that it would prevent friction between clubs. Broughton 12,ingers proposal that referees should be paid a guinea was agreed to. The election of the committees resulted as follows : Lancashire.—Messrs J Platt (Oldham), J H Houghton (St. Helens), J Counsell (Wigan), and NV H Cawley (Runcorn). Yorkshire..—Messrs J B Cooke (Wakefield), J Wood (Leeds), J Wilson (Kingston Rovers), and J Clifford (Huddersfield).

IMMIGRATION AND COLONIZ kTION. The total immigration to Canada during the fiscal year ended March 31st, 1912, was 3:11,237 compared with 311,081 in the previous year, an increase of 14 per cent. Of the total arrivals during the year 215,138 were at ocean ports as compared with 183,817 in the previous year, There was an increase in the number of arrivals from the United States from 121,151 for the fiscal year 1910-11 to 133,710 in the fiscal year 1911-12.

BOWLING NOTES.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT ASSOCIA.TI9N.

The fine old g;;;e-of-1;;;I's—lia's --n-0.--m-ore ardent devotees than Warringtonians, and despite the fact that the weather conditions were far from ideal on Satur- day, the various competitions were continued without a hitch. The second round of the Warrington and District Association's competition and the consolation competiti,n were commenced, and the draw proved most interesting. he finest win of the afternoon in connection with these contests was that of Peuketh Tannery who, in the consolation competition defeated the senior Conservatives by 73 points. In the second round competition Rylands visited the Whitecross green and came away with a five point victory. Fearn- head were not so fortunate on the Bull's Head gresn for they lost by 58 points, but no doubt they will make amends on the next match at home. Cemetery No. 1 and Junior Conservatives received byes in the second round, as also did the King and Queen, Cemetery No. 2 and the Liberal Club in the Consola- tion cempetition.

RYLAND:3 V. WHITECROSS. This match proved to be a battle of the giants and provided the keenest tussle of the day. The old time phrase " when Greek meets Greek" can be well applied in this case where wireworker met wireworker and the contest was the keenest seen for many a long day. The Whitecross men had a slight advantage i,ver their opponents in their knowledge of the land but the Ilylands men played with the same confichnce as if they weie on their own ground.

H Williams made a god start for the Chu,ch-street team when he disposed ~f J. Highatn by 21-17. Elighsin led off well and led by 7-0 at the 4th end. At the 11th end he had a lead of 113-7 but before he could score another point Williams had run 'his points to 18 and thus led by one. With three singles in the next three lengths Williams ran out the winner by four points. The game had been a keenly contested one and as will be seen the result was ia doubt up to the last four ends.

• W Jones (Whitecross), equalised the points when he defeated W Cottam iu the next game by 21-17. The players kept together up to the 22nd eud when Cottam led by 16-15. In the next four ends Jones had added jive p tots to his opponents' two and thus led by 19-17. Two singles in the next two lengths gave Jones a maj rity of four and the state of the ...hole game was then equal.

The Church-street men were ten -prints ahead of their opponents aft-r the next game for Langley disposed of A Thornley by 21-11. After having led 13-1, 11-5, 15-6 and 19-11, Langley had little diffi- culty in gaining his majority and be did so after 21 ends. NV Roughley (Rylands), ran a very close game with J Horton and after 31 ends the Church-tstreet trundler lo,t by four points. The players peeled siwith 3,0, 7 and again at 9 at the 15th end. From this juncture up t the 24th end Horton had the advantage and led by 18-12. Roughley, however, added five in the next five lengths and Horton who had akided another chalk in the same ends only led by 10-17. In the two next lengths he got singles however, and won 21-17.

The nExt game was a very Exciting one for W Langley (Rylands) only defeated A Warburton by three points after 27 ends. Warburton had a lead of 9-5 at the eighth end but by the time he had added another Luc. points Langley had made the scores equal with 13. The Rylands man got the best of the next few ends and at tbe 24. h length holed by 19-14 With two neat doub!e scores in the next couple of ends Warburton brought his score to one behind that of his opponent. The game between W Daniels (Cylands) and J Appleton took 33 ends to decide. The Whitecross man led from the first up to the 24th end when the game was 15-13 in his favour. Daniels got two beauties in the next length and equalised the score. Again at the 27th end the players were 16 each. With five singles Appleton gained his majority whilst Daniels had only added another point.

J Gleave (Whitecrosq) was just fine on Saturday and used hie expert knowledge of the green and disposed of E Blackledge by 21-15 after 28 ends. The Bank Quay trundler had the advantage all through the game and won hands down.

The Whitecross men got a severe set back in the next game when H Langley (Rylands) disposed of S Massey by 21-7. The game only lasted 23 ends and the Church-street man showed superior play all through the game. Warburton got well away from this point and won by 21-13.

-- 11. Eyes (Ryland's) had all his work cut out to beat W Gleave but he did so after 28 ends by 21-16. When the last two men were on the green the Church-street men had a lead of seven points and this game between Stringer (Rylands) and W Ball was eagerly watched. Again at the 25thend the players peeled with 18. The Church-street men should have an easier game at home to-day, but they will have no sweet thing on for Whitecross are determined to fight hard for honours aud one is not to be surprised if they win.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT FEDERATION OF TONTINES.

HANDICAP PROMOTED. FIRST ROUND.

The above Federation has promoted a bowling kandi- cap which commences to-day and will be continued next Saturday. All the 65 societies affiliated have the option of nominating two players. The first round will occupy to-day and the Bank Quay green, which has been secured, will be open for competitors who are other- wise engaged, until 6.30. VETERANS OF THE NATIONAL RESERVE REVIEWED BY THE KING.

In Hyde Park, on Saturday. the King, accompanied by the Queen and Princess Mary, reviewed the veterans of the National Reserve (London Division).

Over 26.000 men of this 'new force paraded, and almost every class and profession was

represented in the ranks. All the men have served in the Army, the Navy, the Volunteer Force, or in the Territorial, and are -willing to serve again if called upon in a national emergency.

The two marine firemen w'llo made the thrilling ride on the outside of the Scotch express from London to Crewe were at Crewe on Monday discharged, the magistrate remarking that they must have suffered great punishment, and it was marvellous they were alive to tell the tale.

A PROSPEROUS BUILDING SOCIETY.

The Warrington Workingmen's Permanent Benefit Building Society has now been in existence for forty two years and continues to be in a flourishing condition. Another successful year was under review at the annual general meeting, held in the Golborne-street Baptist Church on fuesday evening, the old schoolroom in which the meetings bad been held for many years not being available, as new premises are being erected. Mr J W Parker occupied the chair and there was a large attendance of members and officials, including Mr Josiah Longland ,',solicitor) and Mr Peter Dalton (secretary). At the outset the CHAIRMAN said that they were keeping well within bounds although the year had been a very exceptional one. There had been national diffi- culties which the society had not had to face before. However, despite that fact the managers could well recommend the payment of a dividend of 4 per cent and be thought that spoke for itself. (Hear, hear.) The number of members was 276, compared with 290 last year. The great asset of the society was the Secretary, and not the Chairman pro tem., and to him the society was indebted for being in snob a sound position. He hoped Mr. Dalton's health would remain good for many years, and that he would be able to still further their interests. (Hear, hear.) The SECRETARY then submitted the annual annual report which stated that during the year the sum of £1,588t6s 2d bad been received as subscriptions; £3,024 6s 3d for re-payments ; banker's interest, £ll 5s 7d, making the total receipts £4,638 Gs id; the work- ing expenses being £B3 6s 3d. The withdrawals amounted to £1,693 Its 7d. The sum of £2,175 had been advanced to members. There were 224 subscribers, holding 295 shares ; and 52 borrowers, holding 240 shares ; making 276 members, holding 535 shares. The bonus amounted to £3BO 12s Gd on the amount lent, of which sum £69 12s was divided as profit, and £3ll Os Gd carried to the prospective interest account. The total amovnt remaining owing upon mortgages was £12,165 19s lid, of which only £11,005 us could be regarded as capital, the balance of £1,460 8s lid, representing the proportionate amount of interest thereon, calculated for the number of years the several mortgages have still to run. The profits for the year, including £1 6s Id from last account, after deducting working expenses, amounted to £438 15s 4d, out of which the managers recommended a dividend of 4 per cent, which would take £426 10s 2d, leaving a balance of £l2 5s 2d, which they directed be carried forward to next year's account. The total amount owing to the society on mortgages was £12,465 19s 11d. The valuation of the property upon which the advances had been made was £24.000. The whole of the managers retired. Messrs Harding, Hope, Baxter, W H Wallington, William Charnock and .1 W Parker were eligible, and offered themselves for re-election. On the proposition of Mr. WOMMERSLEY seconded by Mr. BATE the report was adopted. Mr. EDWARD WALLINGTON proposed the payment of a dividend of 4 per cent. He thought, taking every- thing into consideration, that the shareholders had every reason to be satisfied with the result of the .year's work. .

This motion was unanimously agreed to. The retiring managers were re-elected as well as Messrs. Robert Lee and T 1' Wallington as surveyors, and Messrs A W Howard and George Armstrong as auditors. The CHAIRMAN said the next business was the con- sideration of the salary of the managers. It was not so much as that of the Prime Minister but every little helped. (Laughter). Mr. EDWARD WALLINGTON said that having been a manager for a number of years, he knew the work they • had to do. He proposed that they be paid at the rate of Is. per member per meeting. It would be one way of guaranteeing their attendance at meetings. Mr. WOMMERSLEY seconded, and the motion was unanimously carried. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded Mr Parker for presiding, on the proposition of Mr W H WALLINGTox, seconded by Mr UHARNOCK.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
CUERDLEY SHOOTING CHARGE.

Jury Stops Case. At the Liverpool Assizes on Wednesday, before Mr. Justice Bucknill, William Harrison (24), farm servant, pleaded not guilty to the charge of feloniously shoot- ing at Coerdley on the 21st April last. Mr. Greaves Lord appeared for the prosecution, and Mr Rigby Swift defended. The facts were a little curious, explained Mr Lord. William Griffin, who was wounded, was one of four men who met together on Saturday night, April 20th, in Widnes, apparently with the common purpose of steal- ing potatoes irom the farm of prisoner's father. They went there, and between one and two o'clock next morning, while engaged in bagging the vegetables, two shots were fired and Griffin was struck. From a statement made by the prisoner, it appeared that Harrison, being apprehensive that thefts might be made from his father's farm, walked around the place carrying his gun. He was accompanied by his dog. One of the potatoe-stealers heard the animal growl, and the shots were fired immediately afterwards. William Griffin was struck by the first shot, and Anthony Griffin by the second. The two other men ran away, and as they were running they heard Harris )n shout "Stop, or I'll shot you dead." Subsequently the wounded men were taken to the house of prisoner's father, and their injuries attended to. At the police station prisoner first said he must have lost his bead, and afterwards when taken into custody, he said the gun hal gone off accidentally. At the police court he said the bdrking of the dog seemed to have frightened him, and something happened which made the gun go off. He suggested that both barrels were fired practically instantaneously. Evidence in support having been given, the jury stopped the case,iutimating that they were agreed that the gun was fired accidentally. They therefore found a verdict of " Not guilty." Ills Lordship, in discharging Harrison, expressed his agreement with the view taken by the jury.

LORD HALDANE'S DEEDS.

"The Times" military correspondent, in an appre- ciation of Lord Haldane, says : His translation from the War Office to another sphere of public usefulness will be received with genoine regret by all those who have laboured with him during six and a half eventful years, as well as by that very numerous, if silent, portion of the community wLich admires good sense and moderation in its public men. Everything, or almost everything, Lord Haldane set out to do when he took office be has accomplished. He has made the General Staff a power in the Army and throughout the Empire. He has perfected the expeditionary force, and backed it with the Special Reserve. He has created the Territorial Force, has initiated officers' training corps, and has been a kindly friend and adviser to those private citizens to whose energy and enthusiasm is due the National Reserve. More important, perhaps, than all these things, be has restored public confidence in the War Office by wise and providential administra- tion, and by hi s own personal efforts has spread throughout the land the doctrine of service for the COII2IIIOEI Cause.

The King's visit to Lancashire next year was discussed at Monday's meeting of the Blackpool Town Council and the Mayor (Councillor Collins) and Alderman John Bickerstaffe were appointed to approach Lord Derby with a view to Blackpool being included among the laces visited.

THE SPOT SHOP

SAM Ng GREEN (FISH MARKET.) FOR EVERYTHING.

• Mr Asquith has come THE COMMERCE back into the thick of OF THE EMPIRE. the work from his Eastern trip. He has obviously acquired new energy. Nobody who does not believe in incessant mental and bodily activity as a health cure would desire to be Prime Minister in these strenuous days. But anyone to whom the position comes as a natural Acknowledgment of great capaqity for public affairs must find some alleviation of the incessant burden of his duties if he can make an important and finely suggestive speech in the course of five minutes, which is what Mt ASQUITH did on Tuesday. The Prime Minister is the' honorary President of the Congress of the Chambers of Commerce of the British Empire. This body consists mainly of delegates from all the chief centres of commerce in the Empire. It has a programme which extends from the reform of the calendar to questions of national defence, and it met on Tuesday in the London Guildhall, under the chairman- ship of LORD DESBOROUGH, who is probably the chairman of more societies which are influencing the practical life of the country than any other member of the Peerage. Mn ASQUITH, the busiest man in thetworld, went down to the City of London in his stride, and delivered a brief speech, which could not have been more to the point if it had lasted an hour or more.

Of course, the speech was entirely non- political. If we are not inveterate and insensate Tariff Reformers we do not carry our politics into assemblies which are con- cerned for the most part with the commercial relations of the Empire. As the PRIME MINISTER said, we recognise in such a gathering together from all parts of the Empire of representatives and accredited delegates of commerce and industry one of the most striking proofs and one of the surest safeguards of our Imperial loyalty, unity, and strength. The object of such a gathering, he said, whatever differences of opinion there may be, are the furtherance of the material interests and the drawing closer of the bonds of sympathy which unite the various communities subject to the King. It was not possible, under the circumstances, for MR ASQUITH to say anything about the preposterous misrepresentations that our Colonies require to be bound to us by special Preferences in trade, but he quoted figures which entirely disposed of any idea of that sort. He said, for instance, that for several years past nearly one quarter of the imports of the United Kingdom have been received from the oveiseas dominions and posessions, and that over one-third of our exports have been sent to them. This means more intimate relations and a rapidly growing trade.

Among those who are attending the Con- gress is the HON. G. E. FORSTER, the Canadian Minister of Trade and Commerce, who has come to England as a member of the Royal Committee on Imperial Trade. If things were what they are said to be by MR. CHAMBERLAIN and his followers he would have had some observations to make, in an

interview which took place on Tuesday, on a certain mythological" offer" to the mother country. It must seem strange to the Tariff Reformers that, so far from mentioning the " offer " which has always been represented as a threat, he remarked that the resolution of the Imperial Conference itself and the terms of the reference do not contemplate any pronouncement upon or recommendations with regard to financial policy or legislation. The simple fact is that since the death of MR. RICHARD SEDDON, who enter- tained the singular opinion that our imports are paid for in what ROBERT LOUIS STEVENSON called "good, solid, minted quids," Colonial statesmen do not talk about the relations between ourselves and our dominions beyond the seas as being in any way dependent upon an Imperial preference. The poor imposture has imposed itself on large numbers of people in this country—not on the men who are managing our Imperial possessions. Those are willing, of course, to take for those whom they represent anything that they could get. They will refuse nothing. But they demand nothing. There was never anything at all in the shape of the Chamberlain" offer," and the idea that the maintenance of our Free Trade system hampers or impedes in any way the com- mercial relations between ourselves and our kin beyond the seas is simply blown to the winds by the figures quoted by MR ASQUITH.

Mr. A. H. Crosfield has MR. CROSFIELD taken a prominent part AND LABOUR in a conference of repre- LEGISLATION. sentatives of the lead- ing industrial countries held in London this week, in connection with the International Association for Labour Legislation. His interest in the emin- ently practical work which that organi- sation is achieving in the labour world is well-known and he, with Sir Oliver Lodge, has for a considerable time been serv- ing on a special commission applied to con- sider the question of working hours and the feasibility of reducing them by international agreement. At this week's conference Mr. Crosfield submitted a striking paper, setting forth a plea for the eight hours' day in continuous processes. His most interesting conclusions had reference to the leading industry of this district—the iron and steel trade—and he made the important proposal that, after three to five years' notice, the eight hours' shift should be introduced universally throughout the whole of this industry in Europe and the North American continent. Mr. Crosfield argued the ques- tion in some detail, and addressed himself to the difficulty of increased cost of production which is always raised when reforms of working conditions are brought forward. After considering special reports from the various national sections, the Conference decided that an eight hours' day in the iron and steel trade was not only very necessary but practicable. Mr. Crosfield has been elected a member of the Executive Committee of the Association.

A farmer writes : THE FARMER'S "Many farmers are POINT OF VIEW. anxious to have the 2s. duty on imported corn, and they overlook the fact that they are buyers as well as sellers of corn, and that any increase in the price of foreign corn will make the production of meat more difficult. The present price of grinding barley is 335. per qr. A 2s. duty on this is, roughly, 6 per cent. Now, it is proposed to tax imported meat 5 per cent., so that clearly the balance is on the wrong side. Of course,barley is not used very extensively in the production of beef, but it is largely used in the production of mutton. And it must be remembered that, apart from any other taxes whatever, when one feeding stuff goes up in price, the tendency is for other feeding stuffs to go up also."

In one marked _respect

THE ANTI-HOME the Opposition's con- RULE "FROST." duct of the Home Rule controversy is the exact converse of the tactics in regard to National Insurance. In regard to insurance, they prophesied smooth things at Westminster, and vowed that they would never yield to the temptation of making political capital out of the early difficulties of a beneficent measure, but at by-elections they exagger- ated and distorted every feature which might not be immediately acceptable or could be set in an invidious light. But their denunciation of Home Rule is languid and almost inaudible. In the House of Commons, "unbending Tories" like Lord Hugh Cecil, rant and bluster in Ancient Pistol's vein. But the Tory Party, as the Tory Press admits, has no illusions in this matter. The English elector cannot be convinced that there is any reason why Irishmen should not manage their domestic affairs in the way which they think best, instead of having them managed by a legislature which has neither time nor inclination to study the local problems and needs of Ireland.

LIBERAL WOMEN IN CONFERENCE.

Three days in the middle of last week were devoted to the annual meeting in the Queen's Hall, London, of the Women's Liberal Federation—an association numbering over 100,000 members. All the burning topics of the day were debated upon with marked ability, resolutions and amend- ments discussed and carried in a business-like manner The speakers were ladies, with two exceptions—Mr. Ellis Griffith, who spoke on Welsh Disestablishment, and Alderman Thompson, who spoke on rural housing —two subjects not at all contentious. The one ques- tion that absorbed most of the time and tried the temper of the meeting was Women's Suffrage. There was nothing new to be said about it, but a resoultion from the executive committee, passed by a large in ajolity, proposing not to affiliate new Associa- tions with the Federation unless they promised not to work for Parliamentary candidates who were opposed to Women's Suffrage, made the Warringion delegates " furiously to think." We put Liberalism first ; many place it second and the Suffrage first. It was a great disappointment that Lady Carlisle was not well enough to preside. Her place was ably taken by different ladies each day, but we missed the "master hand" and the statesmanlike mind of the President._

The daily papers gave such good reports of the proceedings that it is not necessary to repeat anything. Socially, we Warrington delegates had a good time. In addition to an evening conference—which gave us a good opportunity of seeing the London fashions we were invited to dinner at 30, Hyde Park Gate, by Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, and afterwards taken to see "Ben Hur" at the Drury-lane Theatre —a kindly thoughtfulness much appreciated. Mrs. A. H. Crostield Was present at the meetings and took much interest in the discussions. The delegates from the Warrington Wotnen's Liberal Association were Mrs Osborne (secretaiy), Mrs Walker (treasurer), Mrs Lindley, Mrs T Locker, and Mrs Hill,

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The,protective tariff enables the jewelled hand of greed to pick the pockets of need. It enables one set of men to get something for nothing, and it enables another set ot men to give something for nothing. Of all privileges it is the most iniquitous, and has contributed the most to monopoly and unjust and unequal taxation.—(Senator Gore, U.S.A.). * *

There exists a serious depression in the building trade throughout Northern Germany. "The cause," we read, "is attributed everywhere in great part to heavy new taxes of various kinds." Silly people, not to "tax the foreigner" when they have all the machinery at their disposal, as Tariff Reformers tell us they have!

Mild excitement has been aroused locally by the announcement that the King and Queen will be entertained by Lord Derby in July next year, and propose to visit many of the principal Lancashire towns. By a stupendous effort, it is possible that Warrington Bridge might be finished by then. A royal opening would set the seal on the ambitious scheme which has been the dream of Warringtonians for generations past.

Should drunken men be admitted as passengers on board our Corporation tram- cars? This is a question quite properly, if somewhat irately, raised by a correspondent in another column. Our own view is that drunken men should be haled off to the cool seclusion of a cell at the Police Station, for their own and the 'community's joint benefit. The Tramways Committee are "up against " a proposition too serious to be ignored. * * *

If the Tory party can put no trust in a "flowing tide "in the constituencies against Home Rule, so much so that at the Hythe by-election the Tory majority, instead of rising, sank, the political meteorologists in the Tory Party are still less optimistic about Tariff Reform. The Tory candidate for Holmfirth has flatly declared: "I am not, and I never would be, a party to the putting of any kind of taxation on food in this country." Evidently the way in which the Tory candidate at Holmfirth reads Mr. Chamberlain's message to the Hythe electors, that "Tariff Reform will cheapen every- thing," is: "Tariff Reform will cheapen a Tory candidate's chances." 0 * _ _ _ _

Mr. E. A. White takes to task a speaker from. the Liberal van for saying that" snobs" have "prevented a British workingman from winning the amateur golf championship," and remarks that " such a statement needs no'refutation." Ve hardly see that it con- cerns Mr. Crosfield, even if it possessed not a vestige of truth, which is far from the case. More than one evening or daily paper com- mented on the conduct of " snobs " who showed displeasure when Abe Mitchell was successful, and joy when he got "bunkered" in a way that was both snobbish and unsportsmanlike. Mr. White does not say that he watched the final personally, and it seems to us that he waxes indignant on imperfect knowledge and little provocation. It is a good sign when your opponents begin to abuse you. You may have heard of the cleftyman at sea who was much alarmed when a storm set in, but the captain brought him down to the stoke-hole, and bade him listen to the strong language of the stokers. "When you hear language like that," said the. captain, "there's no danger.' A few days later another storm arose, and the clergyman's wife begged him to visit the stoke-hole again. He came back with the joyful announcement: "Thank God, they're swearing beautifully." * * _

Sir Philip Sassoon, the new member for Hythe, has the distinction of being " the baby" of the House, and Viscount Wolmer loses his position as youngest member. Sir Philip is twenty-four, but other members have, of course, entered the House at a younger age. Earl Winterton was elected for Horsham in 1906, soon after he had attained his majority, but he is now getting on in years. He still, however, retains the irresponsibility of youth, and his inter- ruptions are as noisy and irrelevant as they were in his early days. * * We are exceedingly glad that the testi- monials to the Rev. John Yonge on his retirement after 50 years in the ministry are to take the form of civic as well as denomina- tional recognition. Few men have been more closely associated with the best public enterprises in Warriigton than the pastor of Wycliffe Church, and the Mayor has paid him a well merited compliment in organising a corporate visit to Wycliffe Church on Sunday morning. His Woi ship will also attend the valedictory gathering on Tuesday evening and hand to Mr. Yonge a town's presentation. _ .

* - Congratulations to Mr. F. H. Smith on his baronetcy in the birthday honours. Mr. Smith is one of the soundest Liberals and most earnest social reformers in the country, and has many warm friends in Warrington and district. On leaving Lymm Hall for Colwyn Bay, Mr. Smith purchased Queen's Lodge, the handsome residence built by the late Mr. W. D. Houghton, so that he strengthened his associa- tions with Warrington while remov- ing to a greater distance. We hope he will long maintain an interest in the struggle for temperance and political emancipation which is always being fought against heavy odds in Warrington.

Cochrane's Brunnhilda, he came in first by four lengths in the Juvenile Selling Plate of 102sovs, and followed this achievement by riding Mr. A. A. Shorente Zebra to victory by half a length in the Empire Stakes handicap of 500sovs. Lawrence Williams, a young Warrington jockey at present in training with Mr. Robinson, of Fox Hill Swindon, secured his first victory on the course at Newbury on Thursday,. when he came in first, on Mr. J. Buchanan's Chauton, in the Apprentices' Plate of 103sove. file race was keen, Chauton winning by a head.

Fall from a Bridge. Harold Gough, a painter living at IS Lil(ord-street, was at work on one of the Cheshire Lines bridges near the King and Queen Hotel, l'adgate-lane on Monday, when he lost his balance and fell to the ground, one of his arms b-ing broken. He was also badly bruised. He was conveyed to the Infirmary for treatment.

Presentation to Mr. H. Rigby. Mr. Henry Rigby, who left England yesterday (Friday) to take up an important appointment in Canada, was the recipient on Saturday last of a gold watch, with monogram engraved, from the staff of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd. (Engineering Department). The presentation was made by the manager, Mr. Thomas Atherton, who made reference to Mr. Rigby's connection with the firm for nearly a quarter of a century, and wished him every success on behalf of the staff.—Mr. Rigby feelingly respolided.

Old English Fair. Preparations are already well advanced for the Old English Fair, which will be held in Vict-iria Park on hurrday, Friday, and Saturday. July 11th, 12th and 13th, in aid of the Infirmary Million Penny Fund. The opening ceremonies will be performad on the Thursday by Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., on the Friday by Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, and on the Saturday by Mrs. Ziba Armitage. A host of attractions is announced, including steam yachts alpine railway, electric motor cars, houp-la, joy yachts, launch (on river), park pierrots, ballroom tent and dancing on the green. Special attention is (-died to two competitions for which eutries are invited-- a doll show and a guess- ing competition. The Picturedr3me. _

_ The attractions at the Picturedrome are always great and this week's show is no exception to the rule. , A picture of intense human interest " His terrible lesson "was the favourite at the early part of the week and on Thursday there was the usual entire change of programme and the premier picture, " A Mexican Courtship ' a western romance, is proving extremely popular. There are numerous other attrac- tions ir,cludiug "The Derby." A programme of equal strength has been arranged for next week and on Monday the chief film will be "The Patchwork Quilt " whilst in the latter part of the week " A Spring Round Up " will occupy the top of the bill. Drowning Mystery. The body of Joseph Henry Kemp, a single man, aged 31, a chemical labourer, oI 11, Percy-street, Warrington, was found in the St. Helens and Sankey canal on Saturday morning. At the inque,t held by Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, at the Sankey Chapel House on Tuesday, William Bate, a canal Waterman of Hulme Lock, W inwick, stated that he saw the body floating in the canal and recovered it and gave information to the police. Police Constable Jones, of Sankey, conveyed the body to the Sankey Chapel House, where Mr Josi.ph Kemp, father of the deceased identified it as that of his son. There was no evidence to show how the deceased got into the canal, and the jury returned a verdict of "Found drowned." A Slum Concert.

—.— During Health Week a challenge was thrown out to the public to combine a concert with a Health Talk in the slums. The challenge has been taken up in the Bewsey distlict and a representative committee has been formed, which has been meeting by kind per- mission at St. Paul's Church House. On the com- mittee are representatives of the Bewsey Ward Com- mittee of the Guild of Help and members of the Adult I School. The concert has been fixed for Wednesday evening next in the open space in Catherine-street. Should the weather prove unfavourable the concert will be held in Messrs. Greening's dining room. The Mayor (Dr. Joseph) will be present and speak. The Chairman of the Health Committee would also have been present, but for absence from borne. Health leaflets are to be systematically distributed in the neighbourhood before the concert takes place and people will be personally invited to be present. The musical programme is in the hands of Mr. Taylor, of the Male Voice Choir, members of which are expected 1 to take part.

Women's Suffrage. On the .whole the suffrage week in Warrington, conducted under the auspices of the National Union of Women's Suffrage Societies, was highly successful. The concluding meeting of the series was heid on Saturday evening in the Cairo-street Schools, the incessant :rain making it impossible for the meeting to be held at Bridge Foot as originally intended. Mr W Whiteley presided and he was supported by Mrs Cooper and Messrs W Baguley and Williams. The Chairman emphasised the need of breaking down the barriers of prejudice. Women, in his opinion, should have the vote because they were just as touch members of the community as the men. (Applause).—Mr. Baguley, in the course of an interesting address, said he was of the opinion that there was a large number of men who were not as fit as many women to have the vote. (Hear, hear). —Mrs. Cooper, of Nelson, dealt with the objections raised against the movement, and in conclusion said it was their intention to get at the workingclasses of the country, for it was the workingmen who had the power to influence the Members of Parliament to grant I the franchise to women. (Applause). IThe New Workhouse Master and Matron. Mr. and Mrs. W. J. Brown, the new master and matron of the Warrington Workhouse, have this week entered upon their duties. Before leaving the Blything Union Workhouse, Suffolk, where they have done ' most efficient work while in charge of the institution, Mr. and Mrs. Brown invited the whole of the inmates of the Workhouse and Infirmary to a special tea and concert. Dr. Pryce Morris (medical officer of the Workhouse and Council) took the oFportunity to present to Mr. and Mrs. Brown (on behalf of the officers and inmates) a large handsome silver kettle and s and. The Doctor said it gave him much pleasure to hand this useful gift to Mr. and Mrs. Brown, with their best wishes for their future success and happiness. During the five years Mr. and Mrs. Brown had acted as Master and Matron there had not been the slightest sign of friction either amongst the staff or inmates, and a pleasing feature of the presentation was that the inmates had unanimously subscribed their small mite in aid of that handsome gift. Mr. Brown, on behalf of his wife and himself, thanked the inmates and officers for their handsome present, and said he was surprised that they should consider them worthy such a testimonial. lie assured them they would treasure this gift beyond all price, and it would serve to remind them of the happy times they had all had together at the _Blything Workhouse.

Reid.

Our readers will regret to learn that the Rev. W. Reid, of St. John's Church, is laid aside by illness, and has . for two Sundays been unable to occupy his pulpit. Mr. Reid, we learn, is still confined to his room, although happily, his condition i not serious, and under the care of Dr. Parker, Stockton Heath, he is making satisfactory progress towards convalescence. Efforts-are being- made to stimulate interest in the coming ses-ions work of the Parliamentary Debating Society and at a special meeting of members held in the Cairo-street schools on Tuesday evening, several important questions upon organization were discussed. When the House re-assembles in September the Conservative Ministry will take office, and Mr Harold Smith, M.P is to be approached with the object of securing the services of some eminent Members of Parliament at the opening meeting. Messrs F A. Jenkins and N Cook, jun., were appointed joint hon. secretaries, and Mr James Ball was elected hon. treasurer. It was decided to change the weekly debating nights from Tuesday to Thursday. Further matters were left to the council to arrange at an early date.

Attention is drawn to the advertisement on another page of the Prudential Assurance Company, which has formed "The Prudential Approved Society " under the Insurance Act, end offers facilities which are worthy the attention of every worker who has not yet com- plied with the requirements of the Act, or is in doubt which Society to join. ===== 0002601_19120615_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
LORD. HALDANE'S DEEDS.

"The Times" military correspondent, in an appre- ciation of Lord Haldane, says : His translation trom the War Office to another sphere of public usefulness will be received with genaine regret by all those who have laboured with him during six and a half eventful years, as well as by that very numerous, if silent, portion of the community wiach admires good sense and moderation in its public men. Everything, or almost everything, Lord Haldane set out to do when he took office he has accomplished. He has made the General Staff a power in the Army and throughout the Empire. He .has perfected the expeditionary force, and backed it with the Special Reserve. He has created the Territorial Force, has initiated officers' training corps, and has been a kindly friend and adviser to those private citizens to whose energy and enthusiasm is due the National Reserve. More important, perhaps, than all these things, be has restored public confidence in the War Office by wise and providential administra- tion, and by his own personal efforts has spread throughout the land the doctrine of service for the common cause.

A NEW GUIDE TO THE INSURANCE ACT.

The Liberal Insurance Committee have published a remarkably lucid and useful " People's Guide to the Insurance Act " by Messrs. McCurdy and Lees Smith. The authors are the Members or Parliament for Northampton. Mr. McCurdy is a well-known lawyer and Mr. Lees Smith one of tbe ablest economists in the country, who took a prominent part in the debate on the Bill .in the House of Commons. Clearly such a combination is admirably fitted to deal with the National Insurance Act. The book contains :-1. The full text of the Insurance Act—the chrapest form of which available hitherto costs qd. 2. Full explanatory notes, which, by aneat typographical arrangement. are clearly distinguished from the text of the Act. 3. Introductions explaining the general scheme of the Act. 4. A table of 40 important questions on the Act with reference to the -psrts of the book dealing with them. 5. A form on which any further question can be sent to the authors, who will answer it by post. 6. A very full index, running to no less than 26 pages, and forming an invalu- able synopsis and guide to the Act. The price is only 4d. net, 6d. post free. The "People's Guide to the insurance Act "is published by tbe Liberal Insurance Committee, Millbank House, Westminster, S .W. The body was crushed almost beyond recognition.

SCHOOLS. The Cheshire Education Committee at a meeting at Crewe on Monday, decided to appoint a physical drill inspector for elementary schools for boys at a salary of £l5O a year and travelling expenses, and a woman drill inspector for schools for girls and mixed schools at a salary of £l2O a year and travelling expenses.

ATHLETICS.

LOCAL SUCCESSES.

A goodly number of Warringtonians visited the sports of St. Helens Recreation Club, and had the satisfaction of seeing two junior members of Warrington Athletic Club win first prizes in very clever style. In the one mile novice cycle handicap with 10 yards start, Fred Shadwell won his heat comfortably and outstayed his opponents when winning the final by a wheel. Shadwell's previous cycle efforts had only produced two third prizes at Widnes sports, his most notable performance baying been the winning of the Junior Cross Country Championship of the local club in the past two seasons, and doubtless the staying power developed in this branch of sport is now asserting itself. He has hitherto been most unfortunate in cycling competitions, and should now win further prizes. Arthur Collins, a likely youth, with 130 yards start in the One Mile Flat Handicap, ran a well-judged ,race. He was nicely placed at half way, and maintained his forward position until about 3eo yards remained to be covered, when the opposition began to sprint, but none were able to live with the young Warruigtonian. who won his first prize by 10 yards in the good time of 4mins. 17secs. on a 41 lap grass track. In the two miles team race Warrington were not fortune's favourites, as A Gregson, who ran well in the early stages, was compelled by a lacerated toe, to retire before one mile was covered. This left C Vose and A Siddall, then running with the leaders, without interest in the contest as three were necessary to count. W Bolton was unable to be present and H Holbr-ok and A Wilcock had preferred to try their paces in the mile handicap won by Collins.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ASSOCIATION (N.A. CENTRE). With the arrival of open season, members should look up rule five, as this rule will be strictly carried out. Members fishing Long-Barn most show their club card to the keeper when asked for it, and if not produced they will not be allowed to fish without purchasing a day ticket. Anyone wishing to purchase day tickets can do so from Mr. Joseph Welsby, Long-Barn Farm, Fearnhead, the nearest farm to the pond on the Fearn- head side of the bridge. Will members please keep off the land on the off side of Norton canal where there are crops of hay to be got in on each aide of Norton Bridge, also at Long-Barn where the best approach to the pond is via the stile on the railway bridge. The report of the N.A.A. meeting will probably be at band for next week and will be found in the " Examiner." The assistant secretary, Mr Potter, will be down at the club 1-louse on Saturday, June 15th, from 8 till 10 p.m. to issue cards to those who have not called for them up to date. The angling last week end was again disappointing and many were glad of the finish of close season. After all the rain we expected anglers to have a good time, but there was not a good catch recorded from any quarter. The best specimen taken was a perch from Long Barn, the weight being almost flibso, end the mem- bers who saw it sighed for open season. It was returned to the water with great care, one or two remarking that they would call again for it. Up to the time of writing there are still several close season

THE LATE MR. W. T. STEAD'S WILL.

The will has now been proved of Mr. William Thomas Stead, the well-known journalist, who was drowned in the loss of the Titanic. He left estate, "so far as at present can he ascertained," of the gross value of 13,000. The executors of his will are his widow, Mrs. Emma Lucy Stead and his daughter, Miss Emma Wilson Stead, both of 5, Smith-square, Westminster, S.W. He left all his property, including the " Review of Reviews," to his wife, and he handed over for examina- tion all his private papers, manuscripts, letters, diaries, and everything of an auto-biographical or private personal interest, to his eldest daughter, Emma Wilson Stead, to be dealt with by her at her sole discretion. Mr Stead charged his wife and daughter to have regard to his wishes as to the use of the property thus left to them, which, he said, be intended to express in a codicil. The codicil was apparently never made, for it cannot be found among his papers, either in his usual depositories or in the hands ot his solicitors. The will is made on July 8, l 9 it, on the day of his departure, it states, for Constantinople.

THE COWY.

The Gowy was visited -last -Week-end both at the lower and higher reaches, and after all the rain-tall round this quarter, sport was far from what it ought to have been. Several dace we etaken with the worm and returned to the stream. Eels were taken also, but not the quantities that were expected. A jack was creeled oy oue member, about and several trout,but they were very small ; the best trout on tlr set board stands below Boxs. which is a very poor record from the Gowy. Taking all things into consideration it has been a very poor close time, especially when .ve con- sider the number of anglers who have visited this water

HARTFORD. Hartford was visited by several members and although the river looked a great deal better for the heavy down- pour, sport was not to be had.

THE DEE. Chester was visited by one or two, and they had a few of their Northern angler friends with them who are natives, but here again sport was entirely off, with the exception of one member who took about five very fair ells.

NEW CABINET MINISTER.

HONOUR FOR SIR RUFUS ISAACS.

The following official announcement was issued from 10, Downing-street, on Tuesday night : "The King has been pleased to approve the appointment of the Right Hon. Sir Rufus Isaacs, K.C., to be a member of his Majesty's Cabinet." The announcement of the promotion of Sir Rufus Isaacs came as a great surprise t members of Parlia- ment. It is undersbicd that the signal honour which, on the advice of his Ministers, the King has conferred upon the member for Reading is intended as a mark of the appreciation by the Government of his great services during the committee stage of the National Insurance Act.

The success attained by Sir Rufus Isaacs, both at the bar and in politics, has been phenomenal. The son of a London merchant, and born in 160, he was educated at University College School and on the Continent. On being called to the bar, in 1887, he rapidly made a great reputation as a commercial lawyer and cross-examiner, and took silk in 1898. He first entered the House of Commons at a by-election in 1904, at Reading, a borough which he has continued to represent. Prior to studying for the law Sir Rufus spent some time on the Stock Exchange, and his experience there has made him peculiarly efficient in dealing with all commercial questions.

ON H.

OOF

_ OFFICERS' STRUGGLE WITH ARMED MAN. At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, James Mackin, at 17, Dial-street, was charged on remand with having been drunk and disorderly in Barbauld-street,and also wtth having been found on the enclosed premises of the Feathers Hotel with intent to steal.—He pieaded not guilty.—The Chief Constable stated that at 5.50 on the Ist inst, Mr Harry Rogers, of the Feathers Hotel, was in the kitchen when he heard a noise on the roof. He went in the yard and saw the prisoner on the roof of the pantry. He sent for the police, and when an officer arrived the prisoner got down. Constable Wood took the man into custody, whereupon he became very violent. Whilst struggling in the hotel passage, Mr. Rogers saw Mackin take from his pocket a knife. During the tussle the officer fortunately got possession of the weapon. With assistance the constable took the man to the police station. Mackin made the excuse that he went to the lavatory and was unable to get out. He therefore tried to climb out over the root. There was no truth in that statement, because the back entrance was always open. Mr Harry Rogers gave evidence confirming the Chief Constable's statement and said that prisoner gave no reply whatever, when he (witness) asked him what he was doing. In the struggle in the yard Mackin tried to open the pocket knife. The man had no drink in the house, and witness thought that he was feigning drunkenness.

P.C. Wood said that when he asked Mackin why he was on the roof, the prisoner replied that he was doing just what he liked and it was no business of the constable's. When told he would have to go to the police station he retorted " I'm not coming with you. There's not one man will take me." After the struggle Mackin said " I know all the tricks and I'm going to murder you before you'll take me." Prisoner stated that he walked through the passage of the hotel for the purp ise of going to the lavatory. The door did not open to his push and he came to the conclusion that someone was playing a practical joke or that the landlord did not want him in the house and did not like to say so. He thought that the best way out was to climb on the roof. The story of the knife was purely imagination. Prisoner, who was described as "a bit of a wanderer" was committed to prison for 14 days. TAKES OXALIC ACID FOR RUM.

AFTER HAVING CUT HIS THROAT WITH RAZOR.

At the Warrington Police Court on Thursday, before Mr. J. Upson (in the chair) and other magistrates, William Linaker, 9, Wakefield-street, was charged with attempting to commit suicide by taking a quantity of oxalic acid.

Inspector Williamson said that on Tucsday evening about six o'clock a married woman named Annie Monks, of 5, Wakefield-street, called at the house to inquire about the health of the prisoner's wife who was lying ill in bed. She then saw the prisoner with an open razor in his hand and he had inflicted two slight wounds on the side of his neck. She asked him what he was doing and be replied " I will do it again." Later in the evening the son came home to tea and about 7.10 he heard a noise in the pantry and on going there he saw his father. He had a bottle in his hand and was saying" My best respects." lie had drunk a quantity of the contents (oxalic acid) which was used, by the son for the cleans- ing of his Territorial uniform. P.C. Wood was called in and the prisoner was removed to the Workhouse Hospital, where he was detained. The pohce had made inquiries and it was ascertained that the prisoner had been drinking heavily for some time. Prisoner said he was very sorry for what bad happened. He had been mistaken in the bottle, for be thought it contained rum. He did not know anything about the razor affair.

Prisoner was remanded on a fourteen days order to the Workhouse, in order that he might be medically examined.

A DESPERATE MAN. STRUGGLE 1N A BEDROOM.

At the Warrington Police Court yesterday, before Mr S Wright and other magistrates, Michael McGrath, of no settled address, was charged on remand with burglariously breaking into the premises 04 James Sexton, 73, Orford-street, on the 6th inst., and stealing 3s in cash and a cap, valued in all at 4s 6d. He was further charged with burglariously entering the premises of John Mannion, of 47, Orford-street, with intent to steal. Inspector Williamson stated that at 11 o'clock on the 16th inst. Mr Sexton and the remainder of the house retired to bed. Shortly after midnight Mr Sexton was awakened by the screaming of his children, and on going into the room occupied by them he saw the prisoner and at once closed with him. A keen struggle ensued and they both fell on the ground. Mr Sexton's brother and son came to his assistance and the two seized the man whilst Sexton went for the police, On the arrival of the officer, the prisoner was still struggling to get away but the two youths courageously stuck to him. After the officer had taken McGrath into custody and was proceeding downstairs the prisoner tripped him up and he fell to the bottom of the stairs. Another officer was called and the prisoner was safely lodged in the cells. When the officers returned to the house they found that an entrance had been effected through one of the windows, the catch of which was loose. Prisoner had ransacked the drawers and had apparently gone in the children's bedroom with the same intention. The prisoner had said that be had walked from Manchester and wanted something to eat. Ile also said in reply to the charge that he got through the window to have a sleep. Three shillings and a cap had been stolen. In the second case, it appeared that the police received information that the man had entered the house some time after 11 o'clock on tLe 7th June and at 12 40 Mr Mannion was awakened and on hurrying downstairs found the prisoner in the sitting-room. Prisoner was in the middle of the room and said" Stand back, I'm desperate." He then asked far 2s. Mannion let him go, alter having assured him- self that the prisoner had not taken anything. He had effected an entrance through the sitting-room window. When charged prisoner replied "I was drunk and did not know what I was doing ; I did not intend to steal.' The Inspector asked that the case might be com- mitted to the Quarter Sessions. MaGrath was committed for trial, after all the witnesses had been heard.

Borough Police Court.

MONDAY.—Before the Mayor in the chair Aldermen Smethurst, Bolton, and James Evans, Messrs S Wright, H Thornton, G Pilling and T Locker. STRUCK will A PINT PoT.—John Forster (18), 11, Portland-street, was charged on remand with having caused grievous bodily harm to William Davies (21) of 20, Napier-street. ltlr T S Steel defended and pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable said that the parties had had a slight altercation in the Golden Ball Inn, Pinners-brow, on the night of June Ist, and as Davies was leaving the prisoner struck him in the face with a pint pot, causing injuries for which he was treated and detained in the Infirmary. Several witnesses were called in support of this statement. Dr. hurt, a house surgeon at the Infirmary, said that be inserted seven or eight stitches in the prosecutor's face and in answer to Mr Steel said he made a note at the time that Davies was intoxicated.—lnspector Stuart stated that when he arrested the prisoner and charged him he replied " He struck me several times before I struck him."—Mr Steel submitted that Davies was the aggressor and Forster held up the pot to protect himself. Prisoner gave evidence to that effect. The charge was reduced to one of assault and Forster was fined :355. including Costs. ASKED To in: LOCKED Ur.—Thomas Forester,Golden Grove-street, was charged with having stolen two pairs of boots, valued at 9s. lid., the property of Messrs. Briggs and Co., on the Sth inst. P.C. Cameron said that at 3.55 p.m. on the day in question be was at Market Cate when he saw the prisoner with three boots under his arm. When asked where he was going,prisoner replied " I have stolen them from that shop " and pointed to Briggs' establishment. Prisoner said he had had" a bit of a falling out" at home through money and he had no more intention of stealing the boots than a new-born baby. He thought he would get out of the way for the week-end and took the boots with that intention, not with stealing them. He also sail that he told the officer to put the boots under his arm and lock him up, to save him getting into any more trouble. (Laughter.)—Forester was fined Is. including costs. THEFT OF SALMON.—Edwin Linaker, of no fixed address, was charged with stealing on Wednesday a tin of salmon valued at is., the property of Messrs Pegram, Ltd.—lnspector Williamson said that shortly after two o'clock the previous day Mr Upton, the manager of the shop, was serving customers when his attention was drawn to the prisoner outside. ALLEGED BICYCLE THEFT.—John McLeah, formerly of 13, Ellen-street, Warrington, was charged with steal- ing a bicycle, value £lO, the property of Samuel Woods, from the platform of Central Station.—lnspector Williamson,in asking for an adjournment for seven days, said that on March 23rd Mr Samuel Woods, clerk in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committe, left his bicycle on the platform about 4 o'clock and on going for it a short time afterwards he could not find it. Inquiries were made and it was found that the same night about 11 o'clock the prisoner had ridden the bicycle to his house at 13, Ellen-street, left it there for the week-end, and gone away on it on the Monday morning. Since then the prisoner had been in 1 Manchester pri. on and he was brought down that morn- ing on a Home Office order. He had said that he had taken the bicycle to Manchester and left it in an entry. It was necessary that the case should be remanded for further inquiries to be made.—The case was remanded until the 19th inst.

FRlDAY.—Before Mr. S. Wright (in the chair), Alder- men Smethurst, Pierpoint, and Evans, Messrs A J Jolley and G Pilling. THEFT OF A SAW.—William Jackson, 33, New-road was charged with having used indecent language on the 4th May and also with having stolen on the 24th May Is. 6d. in cash and a BW.—lnspector Williamson gave the facts of the first case and in regard to the second charge said that on the day in question a Mr. William A lcock, of 60,Knutsford-road, gave the prisoner is 6d and a saw with which to do some work at his house in Rodney-street. The prisoner undertook to do the work but on Mr. Alcock visiting the place the next day, he found that nothing had been done and the prisoner had absconded. Information was given to the police and it was ascertained that Jackson had pawned the saw for Is. 3d. Nothing was heard of him until the 13th inst. whoa"' he was arrested by Detective Metcalfe. In answer to the charga he replied, " I pledged the saw to obtain money to go on the road as I was told the police were after me. I do not know who told met" Prisoner said he had only received 3d. from Mr. Alcock for the timber and he bad put the saw in pawn for safety until the Saturday. He had absconded to get money with which to pay his fine.—The Inspector : It is a most diabolical thing to say this tradesman only , gave him threepence.—Jackson was sent to prison for 'l4 da with hard lah ur TB Vl7l_o .

WHERE BAD LANGUAGE IS NEVER USED.—Gcrald Raleigh, of no fixed address, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly and also with assaulting Inspector Stuart and William Aikin, B,Wakefield-street. —lnspector Stuart stated that at 8.15 the previous night, the prisoner was along with two other men in the market place. When he (the officer) passed, Raleigh came out with an indecent expression. He took him into custody, whereupon the prisoner became violent and struck him in the jaw. Aikin came to the officer's assistance and the prisoner violently attacked both men. Aikin sustained injuries to his leg and his trousers were torn.—Prisoner said that he never used any bad language, for in the city he came from— Dublin—they never used it. He hoped the magistrates would let him go, as he wanted to get out of the place altogether.—Raleigh was sent to prison for a month. A HUSBAND'S THREATS.—" I cannot live with him, and I won't live with him," was the statement made by Mrs Henry Waywell, Orford-lane, in the course of her evidence against her husband whom she summoned for being an habitual drunkard.—Complainant said that her husband came home on Saturday night in a drunken state and gave her a thump in the mouth. He also tore her sleeve and by his conduct had the house " upside down " and kept them up until 2 o'clock in the morning. They were afraid of him every week-end. He came home in a similar state on the Tuesday and when she asked him why be could not come home like other men, be again tore her sleeve and threatened to take her life. Scores of times he had said he would strangle her. She bad put up with it for 21 years and she could not stand it any longer. She had him before the Court at Christmas but since then he had been ten times worse. There were seven children. Lie had given her 21s. per week since Christmas but had also given her 21s. worth of trouble. She asked for a separation order with an allowance of 10s per week.— The prisoner promised to amend his ways and the magistrates granted the order in the hope that the parties would soon live amicably together.

MY FIRST 20/- SUIT.

Having a few minutes to spare the other day, I stopped to look at the Model Suits, that represented the art of London's tailoring shown in the window of Suits, Ltd., in Horsemarket-street, and fell to wonder- ing as to how it had been rendered possible to produce such apparent high-class tailoring at the very low prices that are charged by this very enterprising firm. Fashion plates of the latest designs of London were showing, and it was obvious that Suits, Ltd., were right up-to-date in every detail of their business. Attracted by the really splendid range of materials, artistically draped, I went inside and requested to be shown a selection of suitings at 201- There must have been over a thousand pieces of cloth in the shop at least, and my surprise was great, as usually one has to content himself with a small selection of three or four dozen patterns from one's ordinary tailor. Here my difficulty was not insufficiency, but the absolute reverse, there being so very many of the nicest designs that—had I had the time to spare—l could have spent half the day looking ht them. The Manager instructed me how every piece of cloth is tested by them before being offered for sale, and I was surprised at the undoubted strength of the material when submitted to great strain. "I called a week later for my suit, which with over a hundred more, was ready, hung up, and waiting for me, and I must confess that the " cut " of the coat and trousers is the best I have ever received in my life, whilst the finish and general turn-out of the suit was perfection itself, and I have already been asked by my friends with undisguised admiration for the name and address of my tailor. Many times I have paid £3 3s. and £4 4s. for a suit that was not nearly so good as Suits, Ltd.'s: produc- tion at less than half the price—but for the last time, and nobody need hesitate, in my opinion, to call at " Suits, Limited," my new tailors, and inquire for patterns of cloths of their famous 20s. suitings. The address of " Suits, Limited," is Jubilee Buildings, Horsemarket-street, Warrington.—A. W.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
GUILD OF HELP CONFERENCE.

A couple of hundred delegates met last week-end at Croydon to discuss matters of common interest to the numerous Guilds of Help scattered up and down the country. Representatives bad come from such distant places as Newcastle and Falmouth, while Yorkshire and Lancashire were also well to the front The local delegates were Mr. and Mrs. Ransome and Mr. Will rid Rowland. It was noticeable that not a few of the keenest thinkers and workers in the field of organised philanthropy were present, whether actually members of Guilds or not. There seemed to be a latent feeling that while good case-work" was essential to success its usefulness consisted largely in educating the workers to think out solutions to social problems on constructive lines.

A Garden Party was held on the Saturday afternoon but solid conference was carried on during Monday and up to mid-day on Tuesday last. Papers, followed by business-like discussions, w ere given upon such important topics as the Insurance, Act, consumption, unemployment and the training of workers. Halifax - a very enthusiastic Guild town,— has been accepted as the Conference centre for 1903.

THE INSURANCE ACT. MR. LLOYD GEORGE APPEALS TO EMPLOYERS. Mr. Lloyd George, in the course of a special inter- view in the "National Insurance Weekly," the first number of which was published on Wednesday, gives a message to employers indicating how they can assist the national insurance scheme at the present juncture. "They can," the Chancellor says, "in the first place remind their employees that the Act comes into opera- tion on July 15th. Then we shall be gl3d if they will bring to the notice of their workpeople the amount of contribution they must pay and the arrangements for payment. If also they will urge upon them the import- ance of joining approved societies they will be performing a very useful service."

Holy Trinity Church.

Tae claims of the church renovation fund. on which there was a deficiency of £l2O, were specially brought before the worthippers at Holy Trinity Church on Sunday and the collections were devoted to that object. The Rev W J Adams, vicar of St. Augustine's, Liverpool, preached in the morning, and the Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones in the evening. There were good congregations.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Peter's Church.

There were good congregations at this church on Sunday when the choir services were held. The Rev J R. Jones, vicar of Sankey. preached in the morning, and the Rev %V Hendy Goer, vice-principal of the Training College, in the evening. A special musical service was held in the afternoon, when the soloists were Mr and Mrs Sephton and Messrs Bates and Sherlock. A quartette was also ably rendered by members of the choir, Mr Padmore presiding at the organ. The collec- tions showed an advance upon the sum received twelve months ago.
