Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

An open meeting of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society was held in Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday afternoon, presided over by the President pro tem., Mr B Farrar. There was a good attendance. The speaker was Mr J W Sherlock, of Warrington, who gave a practical address on the words "Who is my neighbour ?" He showed that when religion entered into the life of a man it touched human nature at every source. Several hymns were sung, and Mr %V H Payton presided at the organ. The chief event of the afternoon was a presenta- tion to the retiring President (the Rev. John Yonge), a report of which will be found in another column.

St. John's Presbyterian Church. Dedication Festival.

On Sanday last Dedication Festival Services were held at St. John's, the preacher, morning and evening, being the Rev.J.Pringle, 8.D., of St. Helens. There were good attendances at all the services. In the evening, before announcing his text, Mr. Pringle addressed a few encouraging words to the members of the congregation, congratulating them upon the success which had attended their efforts at St. John's, and exhorting them to continue in their good work. The preacher chose for the subject of his evening discourse the story of Jephthah and his daughter, as relited in the 11th chapter of Judges, drawing from this Old Testament story many useful and instructive lessons. In the heroic ages of nations, said Mr. Pringle, there were always found great men and noble women. Jephthah and hss daughter had lived in such an age, and might be called both brave and noble. Of the daughter they were told little, but could infer much, indicating good- ness of heart, tender love of father and fatherland, genuine heroism and spiritual faith. Jephthah had been the sport of fate and fortune, driven forth from his father's home by cruel and unkind brethren, but brave and independent he became the captain of mighty men of valour: Let them notice his nobility when appealed to by his brethren in their time 9f need. He was a true patriot and too great a man for false pride and petty reason- ing. It was well when men brought their religion to bear upon trying situations. Jephthah showed himself a true statesman and a great soldier. Then with regard to Jephthah's vow. All vows were not wrong. Vows were made for strength and security. A man could not be wrong in making a vow to which every principle of integrity drew hint, but if men were more Christlike there would be little need for vows Said the psalmist" Offer unto God thanksgivinv, and pay thy vows." But Jephthah's vow was a rash one, mistaken and sinful in the making and keeping, due to a wrong idea of God, in both the father's and the I daughter's faith. Both made their sacrifice in what they believed to be the path of duty, and he (the preacher) believed God overruled it for good. In the afternoon, at 2.45, there was a service of song, The choir gave a fine rendering of the sac:ed cantata " Day and Night," by H. Ernest Nicol, Mus. Bac. Oxon. The Rev. J. Pringle, who occupied the chair' presented the argument. He also, in a few fitting and appreciative remarks, acknowledged the indebtedness of the congregation to the choir for their patient and painstaking work in the preparation for the service, and paid a deserved tribute to the value of the work of the choir, and all connected with it, in connection with the services of a church. The St. John's choir was under the leadership of Mr. A. G. Lewaey, and Mrs. J. Snallham presided at the organ. The solo parts were sustained by Mesdames G. C. Miller (soprano) and W. Dean (contralto), Miss Davey (contralio), and Messrs J. McArthur (bass), R. Mitchell and J. Davies (tenors). All the vocalists acquitted themselves most creditably, and the choruses were finely rendered by the choir. At the morning and evening services special hymns and anthems wera sung by the choir, the solo parts being sustained by Mrs. G. C. Miller. The rausical'part of the services, as well as the special song service in the afternoon, reflected the greatest possible credit upon the choir leader, Mr. A. G. Lewsey, the organist, Mrs. James Snailbam, and indeed urion all connected with the choir. Amongst those present at the evening service was the Moderator of the Presbytery, the Rev. J. J. Glover, Risley. A fine interpretation of " Lead kindly Light" to a new setting was given by Mr. J. McArthur.

Lancashire and Cheshire Baptist Churches.

The annual conference of the Lancashire and Cheshire Association of Baptist Churches was begun on Wednes- day in the King-street Baptist Church, Oldham. The MOderator (the Rev J Farquhar, Blackburn) presided. New churches at Liverpool and Blackburn were received into the Association.—On the motion of the Rev H V Thomas (Pendleton), seconded by Dr. Kenworthy, twelve churches in North Wales were affiliated with the Lancashire and Cheshire Association having a total membership of 616, with 101 teachers and 1,125 scholars.—At the afternoon session the Rev Dr Whitley (Preston) submitted the report of a Committee who had considered the question of the preparation of a historical volume in con- nection with their churches. He explained the lines on which the Committee suggested that this should be carried out during the next year. A sum of £lOO would be necessary to cover the editorial work and the printing of the first edition, which he hoped would be forthcoming. If the Yorkshire Association could sell fifteen hundred copies of their volume in two days it was tolerably clear that this Association ought to make it a source of benefit to their funds. The report was accepted and a sum was promised in the meeting towards the amount necessary. Several new ministers resident in the area covered by the Association were welcomed and cordial greetings were extended to the representa- tives present of the North Wales BAptist Union and the Yorkshire Baptist Union. The Rev J Jones (Ruabon), Who, amongst others, replied, said Nonconforruitf in Wales was on its trial, and they were looking forward, with the help of England. to a great victory. "we demand it," Mr Jones said, " and we are going to have it, and I hope you will back us up, and will tell those Liberal Church- men with more Churchisra than Liberalism to do their duty to oppressed Nonconformists in North Wales." (Applause). The President, in his address, discussed the subject of the Baptist Churches in the light of the New Testament._

At Thursday's meeting, the Rev H Dunnico (Liver- pool) moved : That this meeting of the Lancashire and Cheshire Association of Baptist Churches recog- nises that the movement in the industrial world known as" labour unrest "demands the earnest and immediate attention of the Chrietian Church. Underneath the causes of unrest it discerns great moral impulses and an appeal for justice which ought not to be ignoreci. It is firmly of the opinion that the fearless and insistent application of Christs's law of love to painful problems of modern industrial life must secure tor all not only the right to earn a decent living, but also that such living shall be the first charge upon every industry.' He said that the wealth of the country bad increased with amazing rapidity during the last fifty years, and we had multiplied our production over and over again. Notwithstanding that, we were face to face with grave labour problems. He did not agree with those who said the labour unrest was due to agitators. There were moral problems underlying it as well as economic problems, He had no sympathy with the wild language

I,OIID LOREBURN.

It was announced on Monday that Lord Loreburn,-the Lord Chancellor, is to resign his high office, Although sudden, his retire- ment was not unexpected, as his health has not been good, and has prevented him from attending several recent meetings of the Cabinet.

1 ' PILGRIMAGES ,

AN HOUR AT STOKE POGES.

THE CHURCHYARD OF THE FAMOUS ELEGY.

After saying Stoke Po-gis from 'childhood

it fell stratigefy upon the ear when a native voice asked if we meant " Stoke Pow-chis." Whether Pogis or Powchis doesn't matter, we want to see the village churchyard where Each in his narrow cell for ever laid Thc, rude forefathers of the hamlet sleep

Thomas Gray has made it fainous for ever. Who would not spend an afternoon of pilgrimage to enjoy the "yew tree's shade" and aye the graves of the village Hampden and the Cromwell that might have been?

We stare from the smart little town of Slough. Four miles away the Wwers of Windsor rise against the sky and we recall

GRAY A.T STOKE POGES.

But the Elegy will live Lie fresher because of this hurried and insufficient hour, and we get what comfort we can from that. The curfew tolls the knell of Forting day, The lowing herds wind slowly o'er the lea, The ploughman homeward plods his weary way, And leaves the world to darkeets and to me. : lhneath those rugged elms, that yew tree's IWhere heaves the turf on many a mouldering heap Each in h's narrow et.ll for ever laid

_ —.- The rude forefathers cf the hamlet Bleep. Perhaps in this neg'ected spot is laid Some hearts once pregnant with celestial fire. Hands, that the rod of Empire might have swayed Or waked to ecstacy the livirg lyre. Full many a gem of purest ray serene The dark unfathorned caves of ocean bear, Full many a flower is born to blush unseen

Aud wiste its sweetness on the deseit air. Some village Hampden, that with dauntless breast The little tyrant of his fields withstood ; Son e mute inglorious Milton hcre may rest Some Cromwell guiltless of his country's blood. A.M.

CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

WONDERFUL CLOCKS.

In the " Daily News" this last week there was a description of the wonderful old clock in Wells Cathedral. This is what the writer says about it. "There is one jolly feature in Wells Cathedral of which no other cathedral can boast. It is the old clock made by a humorous monk called Lightfoot. It is in the north transept. Its face is a complicated marvel, and above it there are horsed knights who joust every hour in a mimic tourna- ment. Also there is a droll figure perched high on the Wall who strikes the hour with his heels on a bell. Outside the edifice there are knights who strike the hour with battle- axes. The innocent hilarity of these toys is delightful, and it helps us to realize the genial humanity of the Middle Ages which as a rule is found only in the grotesquery of gargoyle and miserere. "This reminded me of another clock which was made for the World's Fair at Chicago some twenty years ago. I read the description at the time and have kept it • ever since. The clock was made by Mr. August Noll, a German clock- maker, and it took him five years. It is an astronomical clock and is so arranged that when set going it will show the times and seasons for long years to come. • The clock is 21 inches high, 9 feet wide, and 3 feet deep. It indicates the time from a second to the last stroke of the year 9999. A hammer is lifted every second, and an angel strikes the minutes on the bell, while another strikes the quarter-hours. A skeleton, representing Death, strikes the hours. With each quarter of an hour another generation from childhood to old age appears. _ _ _

After the stroke of each -hour the twelve apostles appear, bow before Christ, who is giving his blessing to all except Judas. At 6 o'clock praying monks are going into a church, while the bell solemly sounds. Before midnight and up to 8 o'clock the night watchman gives signals with the stroke of each hour. At 3 o'clock he is released at the first crowing of a cock. During the Christmas time the child Jesus and the shepherds make their appearance, Christmas hymns are singing, and at New Year a trumpeter blows his trumpet. The four seasons of the year are 'indicated by symbolic figures. The cuckoo greets the spring, and other devices show the change of the months and of the daytime, the sun, moon, and constellations, while others again give the correct change of date, month, and year.

THIS WEEK'S STORY.

They were going on their honeymoon to the Isle of Man and the sweet little bride looked very anxious. She went up to the captain of the steamer to say "Could you, sir, tell my—my husband what to do in case of sea sickness. He is just a little nervous about it and—What must he do?"

"It isn't necessary to tell him what to do, ma'am," said the captain grimly. " He'll do it.

MONUMENT TO THE POE.

that the favourite Windsor drive of Queen Victoria was through the lanes we now traverse to the resting place of Thomas Gray. It is the poet, of course, who made Stoke Poges famouß. Apart from his wonderful poem it would have remained unknown save to its nearest neighbours. It is pretty, to ,be sure, and the famous elm tree's shade offers a beautiful retreat, but such churches and sweet " God's acres "are scattered thick over England. It is the sure knowledge that Thomas Gray loved it that draws the visitors and in that fact is its principal charm. The Church is far removed from the village of Stoke and stands upon a gentle elevation that gives fine views of the valleys of Buckinghamshire and the distant hills of Berkshire. Just one tiny cottage stands by the Church, a little cot covered over, roof, porch and all with ivy. The poet in quest of quietness would never be disappointed here.

Tiro fact that 'a gifted poet did find quiet hero greatly helps the charm of the scone. Every nook and corner speaks of him. Chiefest and greatest in its attractiveness is the grand old yew tree. Bearing marks of age and storm it stands silent and beautiful as though keeping watch over the silent dead. It was here under its shade that Thomas Gray composed the•stately verses of his " Elegy in a country churchyard." We sit and repeat the lines. Yes, they are all here, the "mute inglorious Miltons," the possible Hampden and the potential Cromwell, and the carved lines that teach the rustic how to die. And the scene can hardly have changed since the poet's imagina- tive eye looked over it a century and a half ago.. _

The poet lies buried in his mother's grave, immediately outside the lower chancel window of the church, at the south-east side; but there is nothing on this tombstone to commemorate his life or death. About two hundred yards in an easterly direction from the church, however, and within a field close to a grove of elms, there is a monument erected to Gray's memory, a stately but tasteless sarcophagus upon a square pedestal, the ground on which it stands being enclosed with a kind of dry moat, and guarded on three sides with wooden railings.

Three sides of the stone obelisk are filled in with quotations from the poet's verses, and on the southern side, which is planted with cypress and other ornamental trees, is the. following :

This mntinisnt in honour of Thomas Gray, was erected A.D. 1799, among the scenes celebrated by that great Lyric and Elegiac poet. He dit d July 300, 1771, and lies un-noted in the churchyard adjoining, under the tombstone on which he ci usly and pathetically recorded the inteiment of his Aunt and lamented Mother. The boast of heraldry, the pomp of power, And all that beauty, all that wealth e'er gave Wait alike the inevitable hour ; The paths of glory lead but to the gtave.

Another memo-rial wrought with very fine proportion and skill is a tablet placed upon the wall of the church. - The gravestone that covers his body bears the words: "Dorothy Gray, the careful, tender mother of many children, of whom one alone had the misfortune to survive her." This was Gray's epitaph to his mother. At his death in 1771 his body was carried thither from Cambridge and placed in the same tomb. We are told that there were but four mourners present, his niece, "a shop- keeper at Cambridge," who was her brother- in-law, and two kindly young men, quite unknown to us, from the same town. This is curious indeed as the record of the funeral of a man who had written one of the most popular and enduring poems in the language. And how strangely it contrasts with the homage paid by the thousands who annually visit the quiet spot. Of the poem itself we may record that it tihn 1i7n42, taken up again after 1749, and finished at 'Nri as ms aunt'sbbre igdu ne here ine a 1750. The dates are those which immediately follow the deaths of his uncle, his friend West, and his aunt, and it would seem that the poem was inspired by the three deaths. There are other memorials that attract us no less. Here rest several members of the Penn family, near relatives of the founder of Pennsylvania, who lie in a vault within the church. Here, too, sleep the ancestors of John Hampden and Sir William de Molyns, who fell at the siege of Orleans in 1425. From these great names carved on brasses within the building must surely have come the famous suggestion that Hampdens and Crom wells lay under unmarked mounds

among the unpraised and unknown dead.. The Manor of Stoke derives its name of Stoke Poges (Poges, not Pogis, seems to be correct) from its heiress Amicia Stoke having nißrrie-d Robert Poges, a night of -The Shire. in the 12th century. That so many great names are associated with the little church gives reasonable ground for pride to the villagers. KlVith the great history-making names of PeWi and Hampden looking down upon them Me village children who throng the church ought to lack nothing in inspiration. _ _ _

We 'finish our brief hour as we began it, under the elm tree's shade. Save for the song of the thrush in a tall elm tree and the tinkle of a sheep bell all is still. The sun is nearing the west and the sky is ablaze. It would be pied to sit here milli the glow has faded, to hear the curfew ring and watch "the lowing herds wind slowly o'er the lea." Why not live that sunset over again in the goodly. company of Thomas Gray? .

But insteid of the Elegy we pore over that modern leispre-destroying thing, the rad- -way guide, and the sun is still high in the heavens as we stride through the pretty lyeh gate and 16ave the thrush to his song.

HOW TIIEY TALK IN THE EAST.

THE REV. JOHN YONGE.

FAREWELL SERVICES AND PRESENTATIONS.

dbe Rev. John Yonge will close his long pastorate at Wyetiff r Congregational Church t -morrow (Sunday), when special services will be h(ld. It is exrecmd that in addition to the regular worshippers there will be, along with the Mayor aud members ol the C rporation (who have expressed their intention of attending the morning service), many friends and former adherents who will desire to show their high regard for the retiring pastor. On Tuesday evening a public meeting will be held in the church when presentations will be made to the Rev. John Yooge. 'rhe Chairmen will be Xlr Gerard N Ford, J.P., C.C., of Buxton (ex-chairman of the Congregational Union of England and Wales) and Professor E Armitage (United College, Bradford. rhe Mayor (Councill ir Dr Joseph) will make a presentation on behalf of the citizens of Warrington, and Mr Ziba Armitage will make one in the name of the c mgregation of Wycliffe Church. The speakers sill inc'ude the Rector (Rev Canon Willis) and the Rev W Biacecamp (for the Anglican Church), Mro E Parker (for the Warrington Free Church Council), Mr E B Dawson, L.L.8., Rev B Nightingale and Mr E Shorrock Eccles (for dm Lancashire Congregational Union), and the Rev W Cooper (for the Warrington Ministers' Fraternal). Beforo the inceting Mr W II Pay ton, Mus. Bac., will give an organ recital, The Rev. John Yonge has now completed fifty years' Eel vice in the Congregational ministry. In 1866 he settled at Heywood, and his pastorate there, a succetasful one, extended over a period of ten years. On the sth November, 1876, he succeeded the late Rev. George Sale Reaney as pastor of the Wycliffe Church in Bewsey-street. He was qnite a stranger to the town, but since that time be has, by his con- scientious and devoted work, won the love and respect of the people generally. He was educated at Cheshunt College. For many years he has acted as secretary to the Liverpool district of the Lancashire Congre- gational Union, and an evidence of the great apprecia- tion in which he ii held by his brethren in the mini-try is afforded by the honour conferred upon him in 1890, when he was elected to the presidency of the County Union. He has also been president of the Congregational Boards of Manchester and Liverpool. His son, Mr. J. A. Yonge, is headmaster of Silcoates Sohool, Wakefield. The Rev John Yonge has thrown himself whole-heartediy into every good movement inaugurated in Warrington—sccial, educa- tional and religious. He has for many years been a conspicuous member nf the Temperance Union and an active member of the Infirmary Board of Manage- ment. He also served for Eeveral years on the Wariington Education Committee. An earnest and powerful preacher, he bas never failed to express his views with the utmost frankness and many of the buining questions cf the day have been ably and fear- lessly treated by him from the pulpit. All who have been asscciated with him in any capacity have admired his ( xcelleut work and his personal characteristics and they will join in wishing him happiness in his well-earned retirement.

PRESENTATION FROM IHE P.S.A.

The Pioneer of Bible Classes.

The esteem in which the Rev John Yonge is held by the members of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Society of which 1 he has been president from its formation nineteen years ago, found expression on Sunday in the presentation of a purse of gold, accompanied by an addre,s placing On record appreciation of his services and wishing him happiness in his retirement. The meeting was an open one and was largely attended by members, their wives and friends. Mr B Farrar (president pro-tern) presided and was supp ated by Messrs W Beswick, U Wallington, W H Payton, II Osborne and others. The first speaker was Mr W Beswice, who said they had assembled that afternoon, eith love in their hearts, to do homage to one who bad been president of the society for nineteen years and had piloted and guided them in the right way. , They were not indulging in pomp or show but wished ' sincerely to expre3s their deep fr.endship and aff -ction ' for Mr. Yenge. Mr. G. MANE took as the subject of his remarks the words Every man's work stall be made manifest." Mr. Yenge had always been a great worker and in the P.S.A. Society they had some token of his efforts in a greater or lesser degree. It must be a great pleasure toehins to see the society prosperous. It had been a delight to set under Mr. Yonge 's chairmanship at com- mittee meetings becau;e he had immediately tackled the business in hand and as soon as the work was over he went home. (Laughter.) They wished their retiring president comfort and joy in his declining years, and felt sure that at the last he would hear the Blessed Master say " Well done." Mr. A. HEALFX referred to the fact that Mr. Yonge and he began their connection with the society on the same Sunday and had continued to attend the meetings ever since. He (the speaker) never felt more pleased to join in the recognition of good services than he did on the present occasion, but he could not restrain a feeling of sorrow that the time had come to say "Good- bye." Mr. Yonge's services and he himself would live in their hearts and minds long after he had retired from active work. (Hear, bear.) Mr. Yonge bad been looking after the streets round about them•, he bad not been doing foreign service. He had been their leader for nineteen years and they hoped his successor would be fit to wear the armour when Mr. Yonge cast it aside. Those who were now in the society were men of mettle. The speaker referred to the special efforts recently made which had resulted in the wiping off of a debt of over £l5 and the accumulation of £9 19s. in the bank. Now that Mr. Yonge's retiring days had come (concluded Mr. Osborne) and he had reached Mount Nebo, they hoped he might have a view of the Promised Land, and that he and his good wife might live in peace, calm and happiness for the remainder of their days. (Applause). Mr. W. H. PAvTors (organist), said he was as old as Mr. Yonge and Mr. Healey in P.S A. work. He began on the first Sunday of the society's existence and had not missed many times since. JOHN YONOE, in returning thanks for the gift, said that what he had hem trying to do all his life as to get rid of himself—to forget himsel f —and there- fire when members of that society spent half-an-hour in ex'olling some imaginary virtue' which they were kind enough to suppose he possessed, and in expressing gratitude for some degree of work which in years gone by he had helped to do in the P.S.A. movement, it was a somewhat painful ordeal for him to pass through. At the same time, he thanked them most heartily not only for the Oft, but for the expression of their affection for bin' as a Christian worker and helper amongst them. He could sly to them with perfect frankness that the success and pro- gress cf the P.S.A. work was always a very precious - thing in his esteem. They formed the society in 1893 with a great many high thoughts of what they were going to do, and certainly it did seem for the first two or three years as if they had realized their ambitions ; because they used to base a membership in those early days of 1,600 men or even more and the church was filled every Sunday afternoon. Things then went with a swing, for "nothing succeeds like success," and es the numbers kept up, a wonderful feeling was created and a large amount of useful work accomplished. Since then they had had to fall upon other ways, and he would give the explanation for tho alteration in their appear- ance as a society that be had given once or twice before. Tee society would, in his opinion, have kept up its 'nu bers to very near the old standard, if it had not been for one simple fact. He believed he was correct in saying that when the P.S.A. was for tied there were hardly any young men's Bible classes in any of the Anglican Sunday schools in Warrington. When some of their Anglican brethren strolled past Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoons and saw so many men turning out, they wondered what in the world was happening. As a great many of the brethren lit their pipes as they went away,ene wag of a clergyman asked : " Has there been a smoking concert at Wycliffe Church ?" (Laughter.)The fact that so many men met there under those circumstances set some of their Anglican brethren thinking—and he was not sorry for it. hey at once INAUGURATED A NUMBER OF YOUNG MEN'S CLASSES in oonnestion with their own Sunday schools end he believed numbers of the young men whom they suc- ceeded in attracting naturally and properly belonged to them, and responded to the invitation earnestly given by their leaders to spend Sunday afternoon with them rather than attend a Nonconformist chapel. The good work which those classes were doing had, as was to be expected, affected the attendance at the P.S A.,but he did not grudge their success at all. What he desired was that the members of the P.S.A. should make up their minds that they would make the society "go," to something like the same tune as in earlier days. lf each man would try to introduce a new member every Sunday, they would see a glorious and blessed result. Having explained that his less regular attendance at the meetings of late had been entirely due to the abatement of physical force which came when a man reached bis seventieth year, Mr. Yonge said he looked upon that institution and the work it bad done with a great deal of personal gratification that he had some- thing to do with the initiation of it. He believed it was ONE.OF THE BEST TIIINGS WYCLIFFE CHURCH EVER rap. (Hear, hear.) They occasionally met with prejudice iin the minds of some friends because they said t would injure ths Sunday school, but he never believed that and the prophecy had not come true. The society undoubtedly was the means, under God, of bringing into a new, bettsr and purer life a considerable number tf young fellovva. who were gathered from the various parts of W,!rriogton. He was delighted in the thought that the society bad never got anywhere near extinction7he did not think it ever would if P.S.A. men did their level best to keep it going, increase the membership and by their presence maintain the interest of the meetings. The town was increasing in population ; the religious needs of the young men and lads were as great,. urgent and manifold as ever they were, and he had. faith to believe that it the members of the society did their best God would make it an increasing blessing to the town. The society would never lose interest in his heart. He was thank- ful to say he did not. expect having to leave the neighbourhood, so he might occasionally look it' on , Sunday afternoons and if they asked him to give an ' address now and then he would not be unwilling to do EO. He would never forget the happy days he had ' spent as their president, and finally he hoped God ) would bless them and always have them in His keeping , (Applause).

" ROBBPNG GOD." Personally, I decline to take either tie self-styled Tariff Reformers or the Church defenders seriously See, for instance, last week's extract hem the " Holy Trinity Magazine," in reference to the Wel h Dises- tablisbment Bill, beginning "It is not Church- men, clerical Cr lay, elut will be robbed so much as God Himself !" Ad such utterances could easily be paralleled by anyone turning to reports of speeches on the Irish Church Bill of 1865. At the same time, one may obtain from the lecords plenty of ammuniticn for effective me against the mistaken zealots who, in their present condition of blue fuck, are sometimes betrayed into arguments so extreme as to seem to me to border on blasphemy. At the period to which I have referred, the then Bishop of St David's read a speech en Disestablishment that has been commended as a medel for Liberal Churchmen to-day. He scorned the idea that Church property had a specially sacred character. No material offerings are so acceptable, said the Bishcp, as these which are most beneficial to man. Ile menticned a case of a gentleman (Sir Benjamin Guinness) building or restoring a cathedral in Dublin, or of a wealthy lady (Baroness Burdr tt-Coutts) building a public market. Each of these gifts he believed to be equally accept- able to God. The words sacrilege and robbery of God applied to this Bill were as irrelevant and misappled as they were irritating and offensive. The thing is true. Will local clergy read, mark, learn arid inwardly digest the same? Perhaps some f them will object that it is too far-fetched, and that an illustration taken in re the 1865 ccntroversy is nowadays inapplicable? It is conceivable, iota ith- standing their own constant use of alleged arguments about which ti ere is a decidedly "ancient and fish- like smell." In his ' Parl'amentary Reminiscences," Mr. William Jeans, quoting Disraeli on the Irish Church Bill of 1868, says his speech was "almost word fcr word " the same as those Sir Edward Carson will deliver this year. " Was there to be another battle of the Boyne, another Sir ge of Derry, and another Treaty of Limerick ? " is a sample sentence, which Mr. G. Cr. Desmond reproduces in his review of the work. However, in dealing with these matters one is by no means exclusively confined to ancient history. A form of petition against the Bill just now in question has: been prepared by the Church Defence League, under the sanction of the Archbishop, and copies of it have been sent to all parishes—Warrington, of course, included. Last week I mentioned a "pop" visit which I had paid to Hebden Bridge, and while there I obtained a copy of the parish magazine. In calling atteution to this subject the vicar wrote therein —"1: am not able to sign this petition myself. Though.' think that the Bill requires (and will receive) a great deal of improvement, I certainly should not like to commit myself to opposing disestablisbrnent as such either in Wales or in England. But no doubt there may be some of our Churchpeople who will be eager to sign the Arch- bishops' petition against the Bill. The copy sent to this parish has been handed to the church officials. If any of them approve of it they will no d übt be glad to work it and to receive sigua'ures and support for it. I suppose no-ono will want to sign petitions either for or against the Bill without having given sane care to understanding the question at issue. What we most of all teed is that the religious communities of England should be drawn into closer fellowship, and amongst them slat uld como to repre- sent real Christianity. Ido not know whether dises- tablishment would forward that most desirable end or not. If it did it we uld be worth any cost and price." I began with a quotation from the '• Holy Trinity Parish Magazine." May I reverently close this, as did the writer in that case? " Adopting this attitude," said he from his totally different view-point, "Churchmen may well leeve it to God to do the rest." And I imagine the Vicar of Hebden Bridge, horn his own Christian eminence, reapondieg with a fervent and heart-felt " Amen."

OUR NATIONAL DEBT.--AN INTERESTING ANALYSIS.

the cockpit of Europe, she had the proud dis- tinction of being ruled by that despotic military genius Napoleon I. I doubt not had she the choice of being able to cut Napoleon and all his glories out of her troubled history at the same time cancelling her National Debt, she would elect to keep her idol and her debt. Our own debt is of sturdy growth and it is Napoleon we have to thank or blame for a large portion of it: the same 'holds good of Prussia. Before the spc,iler of Europe's peace could be caged at St. Helena it cost many millions, and our ances- tors have asked us to foot the bill. At the end of the Napoleonic wars our National debt stood at £887,000,000. In 1872, the period at which the German States became united,and began their National Debt, our British Debt was £770,000,000. In 1900 we had reduced it to £629,000,000. Then came the Boer War which undid all the economies of the previous thirty years and jumped the debt to its old figure £770,000,000. During the last six years we have reduced the debt by 68 millions, and it now stands at £675,000,000.

The following figures and diagrams appeared in the "Methodist Times " in an interesting article by "A Civil Servant." It will be seen how practically all the National debt is due to wars. In times of war the debt goes up by leaps and bounds and in times of peace we gradually reduce it, as shown by diagram 1 ! Year Debt War, &c. 1713 £.53,000,000 Spanish Succession. 1763 159,000,000 Seven Years' War. 1783 238,000,000 American Independence. 1802 537,000,000 French War .(Treaty of Amiens).

Diagram 3.

1815 876 000,000 After Waterloo. 1851 808,000,000 Before Crimean War 187 83,0()0,000 After Crimean War ard Indian Mutiny. 1899 627,000,000 Before South African War. 1903 770,000,000 After South African War. 1912 675,000,000 Reduction due to nine years of Peace. The annual burden of the debt in interest is £17,000,000 and we are reducing the debt itself by about £7,000,000, so that every man, woman, and child in the country has to pay on the average 10s. a year towards the National debt, or in other words towards the cost of past wars.

The two services, Army and Navy are at present absorbing .E 72,000,000 per annum. Opinions may differ about the necessity of this vast expenditure for the purpose of national safety, but there can be no difference about the fact that such expenditure is unproductive and therefore, unless it can be justified on the ground of being necessary for safety, it cannot be deemed anything but waste. It is singular that in this twentieth century there are those who have not grasped the significance of what is meant by unproductive labour. We still hear of people talking of making work by increasing the Army and the Navy, by Tariff Reform and the like. Consider what the keeping up of an Army and Navy means from the purely economic point of view. It means that the producing portion of the community have to provide the where- withal to feed, clothe, and house the many thousands engaged in military and naval pursuits, in addition to making stores, arms, guns, ships, &c. which do not aid production one jot. The men, the flower of the country, engaged in these services give nothing back

to the community as the result of their work. As far as results are concerned, they are drones who have to depend on others for their sustenance.

Diagram 2 shows the relative growth of population, total expenditure and that on army, navy and civil services. In each case the figure for 1871 is represented by 100. The population in the period 1871-1911 has increased 44 per cent ; the total national expenditure, 161 per cent.; that on the army 117 per cent., on the navy 391 per cent., and on the civil services 367 per cent. Diagram 3 shows in pictorial form the actual growth of the various items of expen- diture. The five columns in each case repre- sent the amount at the end of each decade, the height of the head of the figure showing the comparative growth. The sudden jump in army expenditure in 1900-1 was due to the South African war. The actual amounts to the nearest million of .£ are as follows : Civil Total Army Navy Services. 1870-1 70 13 9 10 1880-1 85 16 10 15 1890 1 88 19 14 16 1900-1 383 91 30 23 1911-12 181 28 44 47

It will be seen in the last decade that the amount spent under the title of Civil Services has increased very rapidly, and this iS on the whole a healthy sign. The growth has been mostly due to Old Age Pension-3, which account for over £12,000,000, while the amount spent in Education has grown con- siderably. This expenditure, if not immedi- ately productive, should, in the long run, increase the nation's power of production.

AN EARTHLY PARADISE.

A palace of perpetual pleasure, a home replete with every comfort and luxury the mind of twentieth cen- tury man has yet devised, a place wherein is found cheery light and gladness, with all such things 43 help to make life worth living, including that all-important desideratum of the healthy holiday maker, an excellent cuisine—these are but a few of the rejuvenescent joys for which Peebles Hotel Hydro has secured widespread celebrity. It is as near an earthly paradise as can be found. The surroundings are of the most picturesque description, the scenery of Peebles being among the most beautiful in Scotland. The view from the terrace ot heather-clad hills is such as to inspire anyone who has a soul susceptible to the charms of natural beauty. The delights of the simple pedestrian are almost bound- less RS the place abounds in historic and romantic associations. Glorious motor and coach drives, splendid roads for cycling, capital fishing in the silvery rweed, admirable faciliries for golf, crlquet, tennis bowls.-0 a word there is everything to those who revel in the wholesome outdoor life. The golf course, inhich is a comparatively recent addition, is very greatly appreciated by visitors. In the extensive garage every accommodation is given for motors. Those who are seeking restoration to health will be able to tare the advantage of the advice of a resident medical specialist, well versed in all the best continental methods of electrical treatment, hygiene, &.3. A complete system of baths of all kinds, together with the latest forms of electrical treatment, are installed, and large numbers from all parts of the country have materially benefited from these advan- tages. Visitors have expressed the warmest praise of the treatment received, and of the lasting results that have followed. The lounge, which is so important a feature in modern hotel life, is fitted up with every luxury, and is highly appreciated by both ladies and gentlemen; it forms the centre for indoor social life for the Fuesta of the Hotel. There fs a large billiard room with three modern tables, and all the appoint- ments tend to every comfort and luxury. The presiding genius at Peebles-Hotel Hydro is Mr. A. .M. Theim, who is immensely and deservedly popular with his guests. An illustrated brochure, which will be sent by Mr. Theim on application, deals more adequately than can be done in this notice with the merits of the Hydro,

OUR NATIONAL DEBT.

By C. E. Parker.

It is a sign of a nation's respectability to have a National Debt. As a national institu- tion ours takes rank with the Orb and the Sceptre, the Established Church and Nelson's Victory. No first class power would like to be without a debt. The feeling is somewhat akin to that of the lad,who among a group of comrades each boasting of what they had in connection with their house; capped and silenced them all by declaring that on his father's house they had a mortgage. The boys had no idea what this was, but no doubt they gave it a higher place than a tower or lightning conductor. It is a very convenient device to lay upon posterity the payment of our debts. By the time these are ready to vent their scorn upon us,we of this generation will all be asleep with our faces turned upwards to the daisy-roots. Besides what has posterity done for us? Do not we do a great deal more for them than they can ever do for us? We hand over to them (mainly because we cannot take it with us) our stock in trade, our cleverly devised battleships, our guns, and our swords galore. These they may keep as interesting relics of a bygone barbarous age ; they may put them on the scrap heap, or by a process of heating and beating, as suggested in holy writ, may over- stock themselves with " plough shares" and "pruning hooks." France leads the van in the amount of National Debt, about £1,200,000,000 but then for many years she had the honour of being

MINIATURE RIFLE SHOOTING.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB v. ST. HELENS CIVILIAN RIFLE CLUB. The first fixture for this season, between these clubs, took place at the Appleton It C. Range on Saturday last. The weather was wet and misty. and consequently unfavourable for shooting, but notwithstanding this disadvantage, the home team proved victorious by 24 points, this being the first success obtained by Appleton against St. Helens during the four sears the Clubs have met. The Appleton team now look forward to securing a victory when they meet St. Helens on their own Range on July 6th: This viould appear unkind, sea good portion of experience has been gained in conse- quence of meeting them in the past. The conditions of the match were team if ten men, the best eight scores to count; ten shots at :50,and 100 yards; and five at the" Flying Bird" target, one point for each bit. GRAPPENHALL TENANTS LIMITED.

The first arnual meeting of the above society was held at the Registered Office, 23, Lower Wash-lane, Latchford, op Thursday evening last week, the Chair- man of the society, Mr. Thomas Jones p:esirling. It was registered a little over a year ago with the object of erecting attractive and convenient houses for the orkingclasses on the Grappenball estate of the War- rington Garden Suburbs Company, which is pleasantly situated in immediate proximity to two or three large works. lho shares are entitled to a dividend of 5 per cant per annum and any prolLs earned beyond this figure are divided amongst the teuar,t members. Six houses have already been erected and four additional houses have been started. Any person is eligible for membership of the Society, the minimum qualificaticn teing LIP, which may be paid by easy instalments ; and the scheme, which has proved very successful in other places,is a practical and encouraging experiment in the principles of co-partnership. The houses are welt-built and are let at very moderate rentals. Each has a good-sized garden, and every tenant may also obtain an allotment on easy terms. The accounts submitted to the end of the year were considered highly satisfactory, ..nd the prospects of the society distinctly encouraging. Mr. Thomas Jones was unanimously re-appointed chairman, Mr. Arthur Bennett, chartered accountant, auditor ; and Mr W McLeod Johnstone, secretary; with a committee consisting of the following Messrs J T Hadkiss, Thomas Jones, J Lowe, William Miller, J W Pemberton, A B Porter, J A Ransome and M Ruane. The earlier society " Warrington Tenants, Limited," which has built several houses en the company's Great Sankey estate, held its annual meet- ing a few weeks ago, and has made a substantial profit for the ye .r. The movement is supported by Sir Gilbert Greerall, the Rev Canon Morley Stevenson, Captain George It Crosfield,Alderman Bennett, Mr F W Moats, Mr C E Parker, Mr J A Ransoms and Mr W P Rylands. The fullest particulars may be obtained on application to Mr G H Smith, secretary of" Warring- ton Tenants Limited"; Mr W Mel °A Johnstone,secre- tary of" Grappenhall Tenants Limited "or Alderman Bennett, secretary of " Warrington Garden Suburbs, Limited."

TOM MANN IN PRISON. • In a letter to a friend Mr. Tom Mann tells something of his life in prison. He is apparently undergoing something in the nature of a rest core, and says that his general health bat improved, and that when he c3mes out about four weelis hence, he will te fit p`iyeically to undertake the evicting task of organising the transport workers. Much of his time he spends in knitting, and is rather proud of the skill and proficiency he has already acquired. Ile has been awarded a stripe for good conduct.

Great Sankey.

ACCIDENT TO THE REV. JOSEPH HOWAR D.—Rev. Joseph Howard, of Sankey Hall, Great Sankey, was leaving his home, which is situated on the Liverpool main road, on Sunday morning to attend church at Warrington, when he was knocked down by a motor cycle. He was stepping from the footpath to cross the road when the accident occurred. Mr. Howard was severely shaken and bruised, and the cyclist was also flung to the ground and the machine badly damaged.

Croft.

FARMERS AND TILE INSURANCE ACT.—Under the auspices of the Croft and District Farmers' Association on Saturday last, a meeting was held to discuss the Insurance Act, with regard to the agricultural districts. Mr F N Blundell, of Crosby Hall, Liverpool, was the speaker and he gave a very interesting explanation of how the rural districts may work together. Various questions wore asked and satisfactory replies given. Mr Joshua 13s11 occupied the chair.

Penketn.

PENEETII STUDENT'S SUCCESS.-Mr. John Baker,j son of Mr. J. W. Baker, butcher, Penketh, has achieved another success in being awarded a free studentship at the Liverpool University. He already has the honour of holding a senior scholarship in connection with the Lancashire County. Mr. Baker is a student at the Widnes Secondary School and on Thursday of last week the scholars were granted a half holiday in honour of his success.

PENKETH WESLEYAN; CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.—This event, which is always looked forward to with great delight and interest by officers, teachers, scholars and friends was held on Sunday, and notwithstanding the inclement weather it was a great success, to the encouragement of all who had been responsible for the occasion. The preacher at the morning and evening services was the Rev. E. J. Gawne stationed at Leyland, near Preston. and at one time resident minister at Penheth. Special hymns were sung by the children under the able conductorship of Mr. G. Carman and great credit is due to the conductor and his willing helpers and to the children for the efficient way in which they took their parts, also to the choir for their ready and capable help. Miss Doris Baker sang at the morning service a solo entitled " Little beam of rosy light " with chorus which was greatly appreciated by the congregation. In the afternoon the usual musical service was held,the choir and scholars creditably rendering e cantata in efficient style, entitled " King David," the chairman being Dr Ferguson, of Warrington. The following were the soloists :—Misses Baker, Dodds and Moston,Messrs Harry Carman, John Foden, John Ireland and William Tucker ; duet, Messrs Walter Foden and W Tucker ; quartette, Misses G Bell and M Routledge, Messrs Wilfred Foden and Noah Ray. Mr A W Clatworthy ably presided at the organ throughout the day. The collections at all the services amounted to £32 2s 6d, an increase on last year of £2 2s 6d.

Stockton Heath. PETTY SESSIONS.

The Daresbury Petty Slssions were held at the Hall Cafe, Stockton Heath, on Tuesday, the presiding magistrates being Mr Roger C Parr (in the chair), Messrs J Hepherd, U Dakin, L Greening and W Fletcher. AltscELLAntous.—Charlie Whittaker, fish salesman, 54, Allen-street, Warrington, was dismissed on payment of the costs for not having proper control of tne horse of which he was in charge in Latchford Without on the 15th May.—Henry Massey, farmer, Little Leigh, was fined 10s. for having on May Bth, at Appleton, allowed three waggons to be drawn by a heavy loco- motive without having the unloaded weight of each waggon affixed.—Superintendent Ennion proved the case.—Jesse Evansom jun, ofl4, Solway-road, Winning. ton, and John Farr, Anderton, were each fined 2s. 6d. for riding a bicycle after dusk without a lighted lamp. John Miller, hawker, Cawdor-street, Stockton Heath, was fined 3s. 6d. for using a cart on the highway at Walton Superior at 9.90 p.m. on the 18th May, without having a light attached to it. IMPOSING 0:s1 LANDLADY.—Edward Stanley Rogers alias William Flory, a young man, of no settled abode was charged with obtaining food to the value of Is. bv false pretences from Arthur Houghton, at Grappentiall on the 30th May.—The evidence showed that the prisoner called at the home and told Mrs. Houghton that he was in the post office telegraph department and asked for lodgings. It was arranged that he should pay Mrs Houghton He, per week. She gave him some tea and after that he said he had to go out again and went, ostensibly to work about 6.30, and returned about 8.30. The suspicions of Mrs Houghton were aroused and information was given to the police with the result that the prisoner was looked up. Inquiries had been made and it was found that the prisoner was not employed In the post office at all.— P.C. Holland, of Grappenhall, said that when he charged the prisoner he replied" I would not have done it only I was hard up."—Superintendent Ennion said the prisoner was " a notorious impostor " for he had been charged with similar off snces on no fewer than six occasions and had served a term of six month's imprisonment. He wandered all over the country and his father and mother, who were respectable people, could not do any good with him.—The prisoner was sentenced to three months imprisonment.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIDNES TOWN COUNCIL, A £CO,OOO SCHEME. The Mayor (Alderman F. Neil) presided over the monthly meeting of the Widnes Town Council on Tuesday. The gas and water minutes contained a scheme for the laying out of Pex Hill. The committee approved the plans for the improvement and have voted the sum of £6OO towards the cost. In answer to Councillor Crawford, who said be was pleased at the reduction in the infantile death rate, Councillor Lewis intimated that the question of a nurse to teach mothers the proper mode of feeding the young bad not been lost sight of. The gas engineer submitted a scheme for the recon- struction, at a cost of £30,000, of the carbonizing plant and coal stores on the Corporation gasworks land, at present in the occupation of the United Alkali Com- pany. The capacity of the proposed plant is 2i million cubic feet per diem, as compared with 2,200,000 cubio feet, the capacity of the existing carbonizing plant. No particular system of carbonizing plant is at this stage recommended, but the estimate is for horizontal retorts or ovens of large size, the retorts or ovens being charged and discharge by machinery, together with plant for elevating and conveying coal and coke. The estimated saving is £B,OOO per annum on the , present output of gas. WHEN DOCTORS DISAGREE. CONFLICT OF EVIDENCE AT RUNCORN. There was a remarkable conflict of medical evidence at the Runcorn County Court on Tuesday, when the hearing was resumed of an application by Messrs Bate and Co., quarryowners, Liverpool, to terminate compen- sation payable to Henry Weaver, of Weston, on the ground that his present condition was uot the result of an injury sustained by falling off a plank, but was due to a disease. At a previous hearing it was suggested that if the man went into hospital he would be all right in a few weeks, and the case was adjourned after the doctor had examined the men's leg. Mr. Greaven Ford, for the applicant, now stated that his stay in hospital had disclosed that his present condition was the result of disease, and was not due to the accident. Dr. Douglas Crawford, Liverpool, and Dr. Parry, house surgeon of the Southern Hospital, expressed their opinion that this was the case. Dr. Stenhonse Williams, bacteriological expert, spoke to submitting blood taken from the respondent to the " Wassermann" test, which revealed that he was suffer- ing from a disease. For the respondent, Mr. Lindon Riley called Drs Robinson, Murphy, M`Donnell and Carruthers, who expressed the opinion that Weaver was suffering from the effects of tbe accident and nothing else. Dr. M`Donnell expreseed the opinion that the bacterio- logical test was experimental—it had " Lot cut its back teeth." (Laughter). His Honour said the care was a difficult ore, and be ould defer judgment.

'H

The Examiner, Saturday, June, 15, 1912,

THE EIGHT HOURS DAY IN THE IRON AND STEEL TRADE.

MR. CROSFIELD'S ADDRESS TO THE INTERNATIONAL CONFERENCE.

PROGRESS OF A

A special Committee of the International Associa- tion for Labour Legislation has been holding a con- ference in Lcndon this week, and as the guests of the Board of Trade its meetings have been held at Winchester House. The leading industrial countries were represented, delegates attending from Germany, Austria, Belgium, Denmark, the United States, Finland France, Italy, Hungary, Norway, Holland and Switzerland.

The delegates were the members of the special com- mission appointed by the association to consider the question of working hours, and the feasibility of reducing the hours by international agreement so as to overcome the obstacle of international competition which is now frequently argued as a reason for not reducing, or dela)ing the reduction of hours, even when they are so extremely long, as for instance,is the case in continuous processes. The English members of the special commission are Sir Thomas Oliver, M.D., F.R.C.P., and Mr. A. H. Crosfield, and its secre- tary Miss Sanger. Mr. Thomas Schlyi ter, the Norwegian delegate, was elected Chairman to preside over the sittings of the Conference. Mr. Crosfield was asked at short notice to prepare a paper on the whole question, and his address was sub- mitted to the delegates, on their arrival, in English, French, and German. He entitled his paper

"A PLEA FOR THE EIGHT-HOURS' DAY IN CONTINUOUS PROCESSES."

and devoted special attention to the need for a reform of working conditions in the iron and steel industry. We extract the following from the paper : "The most striking, and at the same time, eloquent, testimony in favour of the adoption of an eight-hours' instead of a twelve hours' shift in continuous processes of industry is to be found in the experience of the men who have worked under both systems, and in the sharp contrast there is between the condition of the people in those parts of the country where the eight hours shift has been established, and the state of affairs in other less fortunate districts where men are still working the longer limns. A man who, in his prime, must have been something like a young Hercules, and who till past forty was working under the old system at a blast lurnace, tells us about it. Be says that when he had put in seven days of twelve hours a shift and then once a fortnight had to continue the last shift for twenty-fours, on walking home after that twenty-four hours he felt as if a straw in the path would throw him on his face; that only men of the toughest physique could stand the combination of heavy work, heat, and long hours, aua that most of those who did stand it became old men long before their time. So much for the physical effects of these iniquitously long hours. Would men dream of putting such a strain on their own horses? They are cared for with thoughtful consideration, which is the more vigilant as the work they have to do is the more severe. Are men and women not at least as well entitled to protec- tion and consideration? Are we to forget what Emerson has so well called "the economy of men " ? From the mental point of view, what interest can a man exhausted by these long hours and excessive strain be expected to take in study, culture, public life and so forth? His exhausted mind runs all in one groove, and the very conversation of men who become in this way mere working machines tends to turn with dull monotony upon mere details of the routine of their toil. Work, instead of being a source of pride— a vigorous and skilful means of earning a livelihood—becomes a mere obsession; and indeed, of men working from morning till night and then alternately from night till morning, week in and week out, year in and year out, it may, alas! be said with only too literal truth: The sly, slow hours shall not determinate The dateless limit of their weariness. . .

As for morality, can anyone doubt the inevitably brutalizing effect of such shamefully unjust conditions? The astounding thing is that such conditions can still exist at all at this time of day. But they do exist, and their consequences are only too obvious, and may be seen, for instance, in this country in such branches of the iron and steel industry as are carried on in Northamptonshire and parts of Derbyshire, where the condition of the workers is a striking and startling contrast to the state of affairs in districts in the North of England, where the same work is done with the eight-hour shift. [lt would be impossible to exaggerate the value of that up-to-date modern system. It has made a whole world of difference, and the contrast between the old regime and the new is like that between day and night, or civilization and barbarism. Given the eight hours' day a man has time for more home life, culture and recreation. Starting his shift at Ii in the morning, he has finished by 2 o'clock ; and in the afternoon may do a bit of gardening, or have a swim, or enjoy a game of bowls or football, or, it may be, take a bicycle and refresh himself in the country. Perhaps the angler's gentle art may take him there, or the pursuit of botany, or he may just go to enjoy the infinite and unending charm of natural scenery and with a view to keeping himself " in the top of his condition." No less important, he will have leisure and opportunity to follow the affairs of the day and to qualify himself, in one way and another, for playing an honourable part in the public life of his locality. Here then, surely, is the making of a happy man, efficient in work and a good all-round capable citizen. To labour any further the argument in favour of short hours and brisk work would be worse than " slaying a dead horse " or knocking down a man of straw, because the case for an eight-hours' day in these wholly continuous processes, especially in an industry like the iron and steel trade is simply over- whelming. It is ah.o irresistible in those industries where work is carried on continuously for five days and five nights a week—or five and a half or six, as the case may be —with day and night shifts of 12 hour 3 each. It is a significant fact in support of that statement that everything points to the probability of the eight hours' shift being introduced in a very few years' time throughout the length and breadth of the iron and steel trade of Britain. But everyone admits that this vast improvement in the conditions of industry could be effected far more speedily and easily if such an advance were made internationally, instead of Britain being left to give an independent lead of her own. And that btings us to consider both the question of the economic effect of such a change and the advantage of international co-operation. I, for one, believe that in many industries the difficulties of making such a change have been enormously exagger- ated. While the average employer realizes for himself, and frankly admits the fact, that shorter hours and good conditions, together with the maintelance of a fair standard of wages, do lead to the increased efficiency of the workman, I venture to think that in most cases employers very seriously under-estimate the direct and indirect advantages of such improved conditions of work and the very substantial set-off they form to the increased cost of extra hands. Given shorter hours, you have at once less risk of accidents through negligence begotten of fatigue; there is less irregularity in work through sickness and absence at odd times; the quality of work is better, and it pays an employer to put down the most up-to- date and improved machinery, knowing that, his staff feeling btisk, alert, and fit, are capable of doing justice to it. It is an actual fact that in some very large and important branches of British industry employers have benefited so much by the eight-hours' shift that they consider they stand to lose an advantage should their competitors adopt the same system. But, of course, as a general rule, the reverse is the case. Employers are chary of making the change, fearful lest they suffer severely from foreign competition when doing so. ...... . and that of course, is just the all-important fact about which members of cur International Association of various nationalities are in general and emphatic agreement and which therefore, in the interest of a world-wide industrial community, international co-operation is a consummation devoutly to be wished. It seems then to follow naturally that the main object of our Association should be to schedule certain industries whether wholly or partially continuous— make definite proposals for the improvement of hours in those industries, and then take the course best calculated to secure legal sanction for those improve- ments by the governments concerned. Absolute uniformity of. conditions in every country and

FOUND. THE GLAD EYE—and it was Mother's. No wonder she was pleased. She had just proved what a blessing Steedman's Powders are to Parents. Nothing else keeps children so fit.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT REFORM.

for every industry is out of the question. Nature forbids it and indeed, seems to shun uniformity as much as she abhors a vacuum. How can Greece, Italy, or Spain, with their "eternal summer" adopt provisions for hours of labour identical with those of Norway, Sweden or Finland, which have a long spell of winter's severity to contend with? But that comparison of notes which has already proved of such value in the work of the Association may again be of the greatest service by showin,i, us how progress in one direction in the North or the West can assist progress on somewhat different lines in the East or the South. At the same time, let us aim as far as we can for the greatest common measure of agreement, for in that way we shall he the stronger, and make the better and speedier progress. Doubtles that is why iron and steel come first among the industries we have selected for consideration. I will not hazard an opinion as to the view the conference is likely to take in regard to these industries and their respective claims, but having had the advantage of consulting several extremely well- informed experts in the iron and steel industry, I will venture to make certain definite and specific pro- posals with regard to hours of labour in that industry. I would suggest that we treat it as a whole, and propose that, after three to five years' notice has been given—and so far-reaching a change might take five years to accomplish—the eight hours shift should be introduced universally throughout the whole of this industry in Europe and on the North American Continent ; that the average number of working hours per week should be 42 2-3 per man; or, in other words, while men are working three shifts of eight hours five days a week they should, in rotation, work one extra shift of eight hours every third week. That would mean that, where an eight hours' day is already established in processes which are absolutely continuous, the employer would then require to increase his staff by five twenty-firsts in order to provide for this extra weekly period of rest. I suggest that this improvement of hours include the datal men, such as fitters and labourers, and there can be no doubt that, taking the industry as a whole, this would mean a substantial increase in cost of production. Now, 1 take it that our fundamental policy in the whole of this movement is to secure the maximum of physical, mental, and moral advantage in point ot hours and conditions of labour for the workpeople of every rank, and the maintenance of fair standards of wages for them ; and, on the other hand, in the interests of the whole community and of the work- people themselves engaged in the industry to secure the maximum of economic production compatible with the above-mentioned conditions. I, therefore, suggest as a set-off—and, as I believe, considerably more than a set-off—to the aforesaid increase in cost of production, that steel-smelting should be continuous right through the week. If that was proposed at the present time, while twelve-hour shifts are still unfortunately so largely in vogue, it would, undoubtedly, in this country lead to a serious collision between capital and labour. But, if it were generally agreed to adopt 42 2-3 as the weekly hours of labour, employers would have a claim to make the work in the whole of their iron and steel plant absolutely con- tinuous, which I believe it would be impossible to resist, and which the vast majority of working men would not have the faintest desire to resist. On the contrary, they would quickly perceive that the conse- quent increased demand for labour would strengthen their unions in maintaining fair standards of remunera- tion, while the increased economy of production would be to the advantage of employers and employed alike and a gain to the entire community." THE CONFERENCE'S RECOMMENDATIONS. After two days of deliberations the delegates came to the conclusion that a beginning should be made with the industries working day and night,and which are at present working twelve !hours on each shift of men, and where in many countries such long hours as twenty-four hours without a stop occur at unfortun- ately frequent intervals. Resolutions were passed that in the opinion of the Conference the entire iron and steel industry of Europe and America is ripe for the introduction of an eight-hours day, and the Special Commission therefore invited the Association to take the steps most expedient to bring this about by international agreement between the nations con- cerned. Industries such as the glass trade and the paper trade were considered, and it was decided that in these and other continuous industries the intro- duction of the eight hours day was both justifiable and necessary. In view of the international character of these trades, and of the competition that exists in them it was felt that international co-operation was essential in order that these reforms may become practicable and may be carried out most effectively.

GOVERNMENT DINNER TO THE DELEGATES. On Tuesday Light at the invitation of His Majesty's Government the delegates were entertained to dinner at the House of Commons, an extremely interesting function, at which a large number of distinguished people interested in Social Reform were present. Included among the guests were Sir John Macdonell, CM., L.L.D., and Lady Macdonell, Sir Victor and Lady Horsley, Professor Sir Thomas Oliver, M.D., F.R.C.P., Mr. J. M. Robertson, M.P., Sir Alfred Mond, M.P., and Lady Mond, Sir Arthur Whitelegge, Mr. M. Delevigne, C. 8., Mr. R. J: Tennant, M.P., and Mrs. Tennant, Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, Professor S. Bauer, Mr. J. Ellis Griffith, M P., and Mr, Thomas Schlytter of Norway. RECEPTION BY MRS. CROSFIELD.

Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield on Thursday night gave a reception for the foreign delegates to the International Association for Labour Legiolation.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The management of the Hippodrome have proved themselves to be excellent hosts for some time past, for the vaudevilie menus have been most palatable to the thousands of Warrington people who visit this I popular house weekly. The fare provided this week is of an exceptionally good quality and crowded I houses are the rule nightly. The curtain rises to Ford and Walton, who are described as vocalists and I dancers. They are really expert in the terpsichorean art and introduce many new steps with a large amount of success, but their vocal item is not quite so acceptable. Dora Mackay is a chorus comedienne with a pleasing and dainty style and her efforts are rewarded by round upon round of honest applause. Edwin Lawrence greatly pleases and highly amuses with his witty gags and songs, and as " Scroggy the Scout" he is a i decided winner. His make-up is perfect n its ridiculousness, and his cross-examination of" a cheeky imp" from another patrol keeps the audience rolling , with laughter. It is a long time since the boards of I the Hippodrome were occupied by a really expert jumper, but this week we have the pleasure of seeing one of the finest jumpers in the world, Charles Mathews. With ease and grace he performs the most wonderful feats ; to clear the stage in two leaps is mere child's play to his topping a p;ano with a "stand." The turn is one which appeals to all lovers of a straight and neat athletic show. Possessed of good round voices Gracie Moore and Frank Sumner score a decided success in an up-to-date vocal scena and the gentleman introduces a blending of clean humour which everyone heartily appreciates. The funniest part of the whole entertainment is the quarter of an hour that Edward E. Ford occupies the stage. We do r-ot dispute his claim to the presidency of the "Face Trust," for he can at will assume the most funny, fearful and gruesome grimaces imaginable. If facial expression is anything to go by, ihe is astonished, amused, excited and melancholy n a very short time. He gives us "the greenhorn and his first visit to the photographer" in a very clever ma. nner and the audience are convulsed with mirth. His many other " studies " prove equally popular and with a touching sentimental song he leaves the stage with the audience unstinted in their praise. The five ernons are very clever entertainers, and with unaffected ease rattle off many popular airs en the xylophone. The Eldons are decidedly humorous and present a story of a matrimonial squabble in breezy song. Winnie Barr, Scotch comedienne, also appears.

TERRITORIAL TEACHERS. CHESHIRES 10 DRAW SALARIES DURING CAMP. At a meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee, Crewe, on Monday, the Elementary Education Com- mittee reported that they bad considered the question of salary to be paid to teachers who had been granted leave to attend the annual training as members of the Territorial Army. They recommended that such salaries be paid in full on condition that the teachers provided approved substitutes:during absence.

TERRIBLE YEARS

An Invalid in Girlhood, Dr. Williams' Pink Pills transformed her into a

Robust Woman.

"Almost from leaving school," states Mrs. Florence Payne, of 57 High-st., King's Heath, Birmingham, " I was delicate and ailing, with no life or spirits to speak of. When about sixteen a dreadful, tired teeling fixed itself on me. My appetite was SC) poor that many days I scarcely broke my fast. Even a light meal left a heavy feeling across my chest and shoulders. I became nervous and depressed; exercise. instead of doing me good, made me ache in et.ery joint, and my back ached incessantly. I often could have cried with the dull, dragging pain in my ;Tine. " Then palpitation caused me great uneasiness.; I was breathless and my head ached for days together: Often I was on the verge of collapse. "I got little benefit from treatment. Doctors said my case was Acute Anaemia, and that my blood was practically water. I scarcely knew how to crawl about, but managed somehow or other to keep going. " I had been ill for about ten years, and felt that I should never be well ; but then I decided to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I did so and in time got stronger and brighter. •My appetite was soon better, and food nourished and strengthened Inc. Steadily the palpitation became less troublesome, and, as I persevered with Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, the backaches. headacheS and fainting attacks left me. In every way I felt better than I had ever been. Soon all signs of Anzetnia left Inc. I was then able to attend to duties with comfort, as an officer of an Industrial School, where I was appointed."

Dr. Williams' Pink Pills

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

EDUCATING PEOPLE "AROVE THEIR STATION." SIR H. H HIBBERT REPLIES TO CRITP;ISM. _ _ . _

On Saturday, in declaring open a new Council school at Golborne, Sir Henry Hibbert, chairman of the Lancashire Education Authority, alluded to the amount 'of money whicb was now spent on education, and remarked that what they as business men had to do was to see that they got full value for what they spent. On the question of children attending school for a longer period, he said he knew the hardship of a large family and small means, but people who brought children into the world had a duty to perfortmand one of the greatest duties was to see that their children were thoroughly equipped for the battle of life. They who had to do with educational matters were sometimes treated a little bit hardly. People would say to him "It is your hobby." Well, he supposed it was, but it was a hobby which took three days out of the week of a busy commercial man withont any remuneration, which was neither sought for nor given. And surely a man must have strong convictions and be an earnest lover of his country if he veto prepared to sacrifice one half of his time in order to try to better the education of the people. (Hear, hear.) They were som-times told they were going to educate people abuse) their station ; that there would be no mill bands no domestic servants, no hewers of wood or drawers of water if they went on as they were doing—in fact, that they were going to spoil the people. His answer to that was that England had nothing to rear from an educated people, but she had everything to lose from a people who were only partly educated. (Applause).

METHODS OF POLITICAL CONTROVERSY. LORD WOLMER'S COMPLAINT. Lord Wolmer (M.P. for Newton division), presiding on Saturday at the opening of a bazaar held by the Earlestown Junior Conservative Association, said they saw the Government using their power to destroy the union between Great Breat Britain and Ireland and to disestablish and disendow the Church, which was more ancient than the House of Commons, the Monarchy, or the British Empire itself. 'lbis should make the blood boil of every true-hearted Briton. In none of his speeches did Mr. Lloyd George adduce a single argu- ment to show that the Church was not carrying on good work. He could not prove that anyone or anything was going to benefit by the proposal to pull down the Church in Wales. They knew of old that whenever Mr. Lloyd George was in a tight place he singled out some youth for prefer- ence and indulged in a violent attack upon him. This time it was the Duke of Devonshire, and there was no more disgraceful attack made than that on this Christian and straightforward gentleman. It was the duty of every man who cared for the tionour of British politics to protest against political controversy being carried on in this spirit. If the Government's bill was capable of being defended let the Government defend it on its merits, but if they were going to indulge in a mud- slinging competition, in a violent tirade against the moral qualities of those who did not agree with them, then they were removing the basis upon which democratic political discussions could alone be con- ducted. For heaven's sake let them keep their political controversies clean and honourable. NEWTON ELECTRICITY SUPPLY.

BOARD OF TRADE'S REFUSAL TO SANCTION

COUNCIL'S PROPOSAL.

In the House of Commons on Monday Lord Wolmer (U.—Newton) asked the Parliamentary Secretary to the Board of Trade whether the application of the Newton-in•Makerfleld Urban District Council for a Provisional Order to authorise them to generate and supply electricity for public and private purposes bad been refused on the ground that there was an insufficient local demand for electricity ; if so, whether be could state bow great a demand would have to be guaranteed by local consumers and for how long to euable the Board of Trade to reconsider their decision ; and whether any other conditions would have to be fulfilled before that decision was reconsidered, and, if so, what. conditions.

0

Mr. Robertson in reply said : The answer to the first part of the question is in the affirmative. The report of the consulting engineer of the council showed that the success of the scheme depended on certain large commercial firms taking a supply of electricity for power purposes, there being in his opinion no proba- bility of any considerable demand for lighting purPoFes, and the Council were unable to satisfy the Board that they would be able to supply at a price which would secure those firms as consumers without imposing a burden on the rates. It is not possible for me to state definitely what demand would be sufficient to justify the Board of Trade in granting an order, and, in any case, I cannot hold out any hopes of an order being granted this session.

Lord Wolmer asked bow the council could be expected to satisfy the Board if they refused to inform them as to the conditions to he fulfilled. Mr. Robertson : There are no standards. Mr. Keir Hardie (Labour. Merth3r Tydvil) : If the, inhabitants and council of this district are willing ta take risk why should the Board interfere? No answer was given.
