ST. HELENS.

LICENSING SESSIONS.

Special transfer sessions were held at St. Helens on Monday, when a number of licences iu the borough were transferred, and several applications for alterations were made. The Chairman (Colonel W W Pilkington; announced that the magistrates, after considering in private, had decided to grant four of the alterations, but, not in the case of the British Lion and the Ship Inn, which would be postponed to the neat meeting on July 3rd. These houses had been on the redundant list some time, and they wanted to consider that part of the business before anything was done in the way of alteration.

OPEN WINDOWS AND CONSUMPTION.

COMPLAINTS OF SMOKE AND FUMES AT ST HELENS.

At the meeting of the St. Helens Health Committee. on Wednesday, several proposals in connection with the erection of a joint sanatorium tor consumptives were discussed.

Mr. Ellison spoke of the importance of the opening of windows and the cleaning of houses. They were proposing to spend large sums on hospitals for the treatment of tuberculosis, but there was not a finger raised to prevent the disease springing up, though all the country recognised its importance. Mr. Vorshaw pointed out that open windows admitted smoke and chemical fumes, and hoped the medical officer would consider if something could not be done to keep down black smoke and the emission of fumes. The committee confirmed the minutes of a conference of representatives of Bootle, St Helens, Warrington, Leigb, Widnes and Hindley, held on the 3rd in, when it was resolved that these towns join with Southport, Wigan, and all the town and authorities in South Lancashire to consider a scheme for the formation of a joint committee under the National Insurance Act, for the erection, management and maintenance of a sanatorium for curable cases of tuberculosis, with oae bed per 5,000 of population, the scheme not to be pro- ceeded with unless authorities representing a population of 500,000 were willing to co-operate, and that applica- tion be made to the Local Government Board for a grant equal to at least three-fifths of the cost. The St. Helens committee also resolved that it was desirable, subject to satisfactory arrangements being made as to income, to establish a tuberculcsis dispensary, and that application be made to the Local Government Board for a grant of at least four-fifths of the cost of the provision and equipment thereof.

Tram Terrors.

Sir,—l have seriously been wondering of late whether our Tramways Depaitrnent has any rules at all which are intended to safeguard the comfort of passengers. There seem to be no restrictions what- ever. for in,tance, about the admission of obviously drunken passengers. As a rule, when the "drunk " heaves himself aboard, he is received with a com- placent smile from the conductor, instead of a stern command to get off and walk. Of late, the number of really drunken and objectionable passengers on the trams has noticeably increased. I can speak frona daily experience of two routes —Latchford and Sankey Bridges—and on the late cars to either place the number of " drunks " is such as to constitute an abominable nuisance and a grave moral danger. Only a few weeks ago a lady whom I know well drew the conductor's attention to the positively filthy language of a drunken man insidel a car containing a large proportion of women and girls. She told the official that if he did not turn the man off, she would report him—and the car was stopped and the "drunk "deposited by the roadside. This is by no means an isolated instance of the state of things which our wives and daughters may be compelled to endure on a Corporation tramcar. I know more than one instance of drunken men who have been in such a state that they have vomited in the car, and I know many ladies who never travel on one of the late cars—say between 10 and 11 p.m.— without distinct feelings of nervousness and apprehension. Surely, Sir, the trams are for the use of decent people, and not for the convenience of any dissolute and dirty being who is " too far gone" with liquor to walk? There is no difficulty about deciding when a man is drunk enough to be a nuisance ; his state is obvious, and unless the Tramways Department gives instructions to its car-drivers and conductors not to carry drunken people, I can promise them they will hear a good deal more on the subject from other regular passengers besides Yours t!tc., S.B.

The National Service League and Health. Sir,—One of the most remarkable features of Press controversy is the consistency with which one's opponent falls into the habit of accusing his corres- pondent of inconsistency. Mr. Dale is no exception to the rule. To prove my inconsistency he has done me the honour to quote a paragraph from my letter which he claims contradicts my conclusions. In that paragraph I claim that military training demands a stamina upon which to build, which the high intensifi- cation of labour in the workshop obliterates. Later on in my article where I confess my belief in a Citizen Army, Mr. Dale claims to have discovered my incon- sistency and puts the question : Why advocate the abolition of the Standing Army while pleading that the workingman is too tired to drill" ? Let us suppose that we agreed to abolish the standing army and have to replace it by a citizen army for home defence only. What would be the result ? All the thousands of men at present bearing arms would be brought back again into the industrial arena. The number of producers would be increased considerably. Ido not think that any one advocating the abolition of the standing army would support the scheme unless accompanied by legislation which would provide an entrance, for the men dismissed, into the industrial world. To advocate the abolition of the standing army without shortening the hours of labour, would only increase the unemployment problem. This of course would be economic suicide. Now it is only too obvious that an increase in pro- ducers, being met by a decrease in the hours of labour, would lighten the burden of those unemployed, to at least the equivalent of the exertion demanded by the military training required for a citizen army. When advocating a citizen army, one must take into con- sideration more than defence against a foreign foe, aye, more than military training. The term citizen implies a free than, and surely an army of free men would not tolerate the continuance of our present state, of economic inequality, seeing that they themselves controlled the instrument of force which is the expres- sion of economic dominance. Don't you see, Mr. Dale that the very existence of a standing army is the expression of exploitation of the masses? Our stand- ing army is recruited in the main from those who have , found the competition of the labour market too keen, 1, and are forced by sheer economic pressure into its ranks. • We see then that the very force which compels men and youths to enlist (there are exceptions of course) is the same force which breeds physical inefficiency and fills our industrial centres with men and women whose outstanding feature is their degradation. All the N.S.L. proposes is military training. I again claim that j)loysical regeneration is an economic problem which is not to be cured by pious agreement that the working man has a right to .' a larger share of the wealth produced. Everybody agrees to that ; everybody symptthises but no more. Mr. Dale refers to the' well to do" who, he says, would be better off if they had less money. He -," argues that these people who have money do not take advantage of their opportunities, consequently it is not entirely an economic problem. I agree, but it is , just because they have too much that prevents them from so doing. It is the old excuse ; thousands of apologists have tried to prove that the dominant factor is other than the economic factor. All such " are doomed to failure. Da you think that onr subur- banite., of to-day, despite his economic position, can separate himself from his brother in the slums ? Fortunately no ! The very economic condition which be creates reacts upon himself. The phthisis bred in the sweating shop, out of which he exscts his profit, is conveyed to his own home upon his own garments. Take again the sweated shop assistant working long hours (often 70 hours per week) for a miserable pittance. He conveys to us the germs of his disease upon the food we consume, whether we live in slums or suburbs, as a punishment for our ' social crime. ' It certainly appears to the superficial observer , when he See s the physical degenerates which abound in our "well to do ' circles that factors other than the economic are at work. These people fall an easy . prey to disease owing to their inactive life ; they are , torpid, dull and listless, because they relegate their 7 activities to their subordinates ; consequently we get two extremes, overwork on the one hand and flabby ° inactivity on the other hand. The one too lazy to walk because he does nothing, the other not allowed to ride because he does too much. Both are slaves of .- ~ one system ; the emancipation of one depends upon the posstoilities of the regeneration of the other. The solution to the problem is not in N.S.L. or military , training of any kind. You may make a man look • smart by dressing him in uniform (with plenty Jf padding) you may learn him to drill and to shoot, you may even exptnd his chest an inch; but if he remains . servile he lacks—" the man." To be free he must feel free; he must meet his fellow man on a equal foot- ing, he must be economically his equal. Given that basis we can produce whit Ruskin hoped for "A . nation of full breathed, bright eyed, happv-hearted men and women; indeed:a Marne England," , Fraternally, •

CHARLES DUKES.

bit of a porch and looked bonny when in bloom. We didn't think much then of fresh air and shine,we took them as natural things, end it is only now, having to come to live in Warring- ton, that I look back and think on 'em. I could then keep my bits of things clean and bright with half the work that has to he done in this house where it's scarcely worth while trying to clean things up. In the country if there was curt about we knew what it was, and where it came from, but the muck of Warriugton seems to come from nowhere. Things ' get dirty without you knowing it, and you get into the way of not knowing and not caring whether a thing is dirty or clean. The dirt is not brought in on children's clogs and pinnies as I remember, but it Comes in by window and door and never says by I voer leave. You never know its there until it turns blinds and curtaks and bedding grey and black, and takes the freshness out of everything. Just think of the extra work this makes. Most women in working men's houses give over trying, we canna get the same cleanness and brightness about ourselves and homes like we need. People are supposed to be ever so much cleverer to-day I than they used to be but what I want to know is, does, I the people who pay for education to-day think that , they are getting worth for their money when the I childer's minds are clouded through breathing sooty air? And just think of the brass that would be saved if smoke and the dirt it makes was done away with. Look at ttie money we spend on asma, brownkitis (be careful of them words, Bessie !) and other throat and chest troubles, keeping childer from school and men from work. Look how busy the doctors are in fettling up those made sick through not having clean air to breathe, Look at the money and work used in trying to keep buildings and windows clean, and with all the cleaning they are always dirty. We cannot have gardens, but we might have a bit of a I flower pot in the window. I mind John, that's my Idaughter's moo, bringing home a geraDiurn in a pot from the Warrington market, and its fresh green minded me of the country and my old home. The plant was in bud and we hoped to have it i' flower by Walking Day but it could not live i' the smokey air and without sunshine, and it sickened and died, and the bairns made mud pies in the yard with the empty pot. Then there was little Jenny. She was a poorly lass. The doctor said " All this child wants to make her strong is to get into the country, she is too frail to thrive in Warrington air, her lungs can't stand it." But what is the good of talking to a laboring man with 235. a week about getting his child into th' country ; where's the brass to come from ? So little Jenny, like the geranium, faded away and she died just before Walking Day, though her frock and sash was ready, and nom she sleeps in the Cemetery or perhaps she has wakened in that City where the childer walk through gowden streeta,a city that like Warrington has a river running Ithrough it, but its waters are clear as crystal, and the air is as clear as the water, and the light is bright and bonny for it comes from the throne of God. The other day I saw some of the gentlemen that manage the town visiting these courts and they spoke very kindly to some of our neighbours abaat keeping things clean. We talked abaat this visit over our cup o' tay, and John who is usually a quiet moo, and does not speak much to his women fooak, had a good deal to say. John is a bit of a Radical in his way,although lately he's got to favver Tariff Reform and Socialism. John says " Aw don't know what they want spying about our houses. We working fooak have enough , to put up wi' wi'out these fooak coming prying abaat. Why don't they let us alone. They only come abaat at Election and such like toimes. If they want to do any good, why don't they take their depitations to some of the grand country houses where the bosses live, and ask them to put in bigger boilers so that they can burn th' smoke instead of sending it into the air to dirty fooake's houses. LA them do their part to keep things clean and we'll do ours." , John said a lot more. He called some fooaks " leather I Yeds " and "numskulls "and the like, but these bits I are not for print; what I've rote down is the text so to I speak. ,

When Bessie toTd her fayther abaat our letter to th' Examiner it made him shake with laffin and he managed to break his pipe. But when Bessie read out as far as we'd got he seemed pleased like. "Send it in, it'll need a sight of fettling up by the Editor afore it fit to print, but send it in, and let it tak its chance." Su we sent it in and I know when John comes out from work at breakfast time on Saturday morning he'll hurry to get a copy of th' Examiner to see if his lil wench's name and letter is printed, Yore's truly, GRANNIE AND BESSIE.

WHERE CHESHIRE BEGINS

It is a gravely disputed point at \Varrington as to who are Cheshire folk. and who are Lancastrians.llany who live between the Ship Canal and the River tdersry fondly imagine they are Lancashire residents, for is not Warrington a Lancashire town, and doesn't War- rington extend to the canal ? But for all that the boundary of the ancient county of Lancashire has never altered. If you t.tand with legs apart under the red lamp on V\ arrington 13ridge you are in b .th counties at once, as surely as if you were sitting astride a boundary stone. The clinching evidence is in the fact that St. James' Church and Cariat Church are both in the diocese of Chester, and the boundaries of that diocese are as ancient as the county boutidaries and correspond exactly with them.

SMOKE!

ANOTHER MEDICAL PRONOUNCEMENT

"Where there's smoke there's fire " is good scouting lore, and apparently our ventilation of the local smoke nuisance is supplying convincing proof of the adage. Though the question has usually been the subject of light and thoughtless treatment by our Town Council there is evidently a considerable body of thinking men in the town who consider the menace to health seri, us and the remedy imperative. Following Dr. Beunett's thoughtful letter on the effect of smoke on the lungs, we have this week a valued contribution born Dr. Manson, and we hope to keep the pot boiling to some effective purpose in the near future. Even" Grannie's " words are entitled to the respectful consideration of every municipal reformer.

A SMOKY SCHOOL SITE. [To TOE EDITOR 01 TIIE " EXAMINEE."I Sir,—ln your last issue Dr. Bennett writes authorita- tively on the evils of smoke. \Voile we await the epoch of smokeless combustion much might be done to mitigate the evils of inevitable smoke, especially on the young, by selecting sites for schools and other buildings as far as possible from smoke producing areas. From this point of view a more unfortunate site could not have been chosen for Warrington's newest school at Evelyn-street. Anyone who has occasion to go in that direction will often see the school wreathed in smoke from the adjoining iron works. How effective ventilation is to be carried out in that school during south and south-westerly winds —the prevailing winds in this country—beats one to understand.—Yours, etc , J. MANSON.

8, \V inmarleigb-street, Warriogton, June 12th, 1912. WHAT GRANNIE THINKS. THE STRUGGLE WITH DIRT AT HOME.

Aw see there's someborl thee calls the " Whirl- wind" makken a dust about the Warrington smoke. I got my grandchild, Bessie, who has just passed her seventh standard, to read it all out to me, and that "Whirlwind " seems a gradely chap and knows how to brast ant. I lives in one o'th dirtiest streets in Warrington with my youngest dowter, whose mon is a laborer at cne of the works. There are four childer that call me Granny. I am not very good either at reading or writing, not that I canna, but my eyes is getting dim and rheumatics had made my writing hand stiff. So I am getting Bessie, the youngest of the bairns, to pat this down for me, hoping that the edit 3r will print it just to show what an old woman thinks abaat the smoke. I doan't know if Bessie will be able to write it down to make it plain. The trouble with Bessie is that hoo wants to write it the way the teachers speak at school. " Grannie " hoo says, " People won't under- stand it if you talk like that, and I can't spell it." "Sithee child I've talked gradely owd Lancashire for over seventy year and aw'm too owd to alter, but thee can put it in the best English Viee can." Aw knJws th'wench will do her best." Most of our neighbours don't moyther aba.aut the smoke, they have come to think it can't be helped, or , if it can they don't count in th'helping, and they have other things t,) think of. It is strange how folks can get used to anything. But I was country bred, and often like to think o'th thatched cottage to which my mon brought me when we were wed, nigh fifty year ago, and where my eight childer were born and reared —all but one lad who was born wakely. Work was hard and wages low, but we had a bit of garden and kept a pig to help out. There was always the gl.anings of harvest time that me and the adder gathered. We need to bring them to the hearthstone and beat out th' wheat, which I made into frurnerty, and the childer liked it better than anything, specially when we could get black trade with it. The straw was used for the pigs, and the pigs were pigs in them days, for they often had to pay the rent, and they were welt looked after. And after tater time me and th' childer followed th' plough- ing, and were allowed to get the taters which were turned up, that had been missed in the gathering. In this way we managed to keep going, and the pig and the garden helped us along. We always had the fresh air and the sunshine and a bit of flower garden in front of the cottage, with sweet Williams and rosemary in it; there was a rose that climbed over a

description. The first six miles is a climb to Windy Gap 900 feet up, along a national road, constructed as a relief work, horn Killarney to Glengariff, at the time of the potato famine, of fairly good quality, and wonderfully graded. We then descended by a tortuous by-road, very had for half a mile to the 0 wenreagh 'River that feeds the Upper Lake. The next five miles 'is a ride to be remembered. The surface is a perfect racing track, and, closed in on all eides by the tower- ing Reeks,the valley seems covered with bogs. Don't imagine that to be a term for ugliness. These Irish mosses, dangerous to a stranger, have a charm all their own. Besides the royal ferns, the iris, the water lilies, the cotton grass and the mosses, there are many lovely plants that are almost strange to us low-landers. The two insi ct-eating plants, sundew and butterwort, grow in millions, the 'latter like big violets, the beautiful and fragrant asphodel covers patches with an orange carpet, and the purple heather is everywhere. We climbed out of the Owenreagh Valley by the world-renowned Gap of Dutdoe. We met scores of " A-mur-cans " and other swells riding down on pony limit. They drive from the 1 otels near Killarney town to Kate Kearney's Co tage where the pities are

The Examiner, Saturday, June 15, 1912.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER T.R. TRIPPER.

MR. H. SMITH IN GERMANY.

PROSPERITY " BAFFLES DESCRIPTION."

Do you remember the "Tariff Trippers " ? Their laughable excursions to Germany in 1910, at the expense of a wealthy Tariff Reformer, provided the world with much merriment. They took photographs of the, splendid streets in which the Berlin workmen and their families lived—wide streets lined with great balconied houses—and asked us to believe that never ,were workmen housed so grandly or living so happily. Facts threw a vastly different light on the conditions of home-life and employment in Germany, but the fiction is still kept up wherever possible, as the following cutting from our contemporary, the "Guardian," of June Ist, brilliantly illustrates : MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P., ON HOLIDAY. A VISIT TO GERMANY.

Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., has taken advantage of the Whitsuntide holidays to enjoy a short trip to Germany. He writes to a correspondent : " I wish all Warrington could come and see the happiness and prosperity of the people. It baffles description." Now if the "prosperity " of German people baffles even Mr. Harold Smith's descriptive powers it must be truly remarkable. The paragraph informs us that Mr. Smith had only visited Germany on a " short trip" for the Whitsuntide holidays, but in that short space of time he blossomed forth into the typical Tariff Reform Tripper, as the exuberant message to a correspondent at home clearly proves. We suspect that the Member for Warrington, on arrival in Germany, took a stroll from his hotel down the principal street on the first day of the "short trip," and discovered at once that the happiness and prosperity of the people baffles description." Great discoveries have frequently been made in a moment as history records, and we can imagine Mr. Smith,burst- ing with the importance and significance of the paradise-like environment of the German people, hurrying to the nearest stationer's and there despatching to the friend at home a picture postcard with the terse but illuminating message quoted in our con temporary. Unfortunately for Mr. Smith, he is not the pioneer discoverer, any more than he is the first Tariff Reform Tripper. But, he might have learned by experience. These fairy tales from the Fatherland have usually come to be scoffed: at, and their glamour has faded away before the strong light of cold reason and solid fact. What he saw, may have baffled description, but it does not baffle those inquirers after truth who have made a longer stay in Germany and have looked beneath the surface of things. They found that the fronts of these imposing blocks of workmen's dwellings were let out as flats to well-to-do people, using separate staircases. The sides and backs, with little air and light, were divided into smaller flats for the crowded families of the poor. Half the workmen of Berlin lived in these back places. Our easily-deceived Tariff Trippers imagined they enjoyed the spacious sunshine of the front! To-day a powerful committee, led by no less important a public man than Herr Dernburg, the ex-Secretary for the Colonies, has started a strong movement for the reform of this very system of housing that our Tariff Trippers so superficially admired. The Dernburg Committee is at present holding meetings and issuing publications which throw an instructive light on Berlin social conditions The town-planning question is being treated mainly from the pint of view of getting rid of the five-storied flat barracks, and replacing them with one-family houses (.4 English type, or, where that is n.t possible, with houses with as few flats as possible, and not more than three stories high. The Town-planning at present practised is universally condemned. The present system is to make everywhere relatively broad streets; and this involves deep building plots; and therefore high houses enclosing court-yards, the side and back parts of which are lightless and airless except in the top stories. In the lower storey dwellings the sun penetrates only when the courtyards are exceptionally large.— " Westminster Gazette " (April 27th), Berlin Correspondent. Thus, all that Free Traders said at the time in reply to these early trippers, the majority of whom were led by the nose by Tariff Reform League guides, is confirmed by a body of responsible Germans anxious for the betterment of their fellows. The Committee's report, adds that a third !of the Berlin school-children are officially• registered as unhealthy. No wonder, when the high cost of living under Protection huddles them in flats, without sunshine and without air. Mr. Smith had doubtless insufficient time to inquire further, or he might have learned that Paradise is still Paradise lost, and not rediscovered in Germany.

THE "DAILY MAIL'S" REVELATION. In an unguarded moment the Protectionist "Daily Mail " in October, 1910, published the following from its Special Commissioner in Germany, Frances H. Low, who reports as follows: THE WORKINGMAN'S WIFE.

Wherever I went I heard complaints of the increased cost of living, and of the number of married women compelled to work at frightfully low wages. There are no women's trade unions And it is curious to note that almost all necessaries of life are dearer, and that the real comfort almost universally enjoyed is entirely due to capable management allied to knowledge on the part of the German women. With tbe exception of rent, which is cheaper, the German housewife has to pay almost double what her sister in England pays. FOOD TROUBLES IN GERMANY.

On the 23rd September last, the "Morning Post " published the following telegram from

". BERLIN, SErr. 22. According to the morning papers, the Chief Burgomastar of Berlin, at yesterday's meeting of the Municipal Council, declared that the city authorities earnestly desired to take effecti e steps to relieve the distress caused among the poorer classes by the rise in the price of food." WORK AND WAGES.

The reports on the cost of living in British, German, and French towns published by our Board of Trade give the following general conclusions :

===== 0002601_19120615_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
wAGIO3 PEll Itouß.

Where a Briton earns 5s in wages A German earns 3s 9d. mulls OF LABOR.

Where a Briton works 100 hours A German works 111 hours. HOUSE RENTS. Where a Briton pays 5s in rent A German pays Os Id. WEEKLY BUDGET.

Where a Briton spends 5s on food and fuel A German spends 5s 107,d. The above are the real bases of the happiness and prosperity in Germany which Mr. Smith found, in a " short trip," to " baffle description!' The British workman has bigger vrages,shorter hours, aud cheaper food than either the German or the Frenchman. and under Free Trade as against Protection abroad he will continue so to have them. PRICES OF BREAD.

Another thing which Mr. Harold Smith has failed to discover is that all the world over (Germany included) the price of bread under Tariff Reform is higher than under Free Trade. We challenge any Tariff Reformer to deny that in Berlin in 1908, 41bs. of rye bread cost Gad., and 41bs. of white bread cost

llld. In London at the same time 4lbs. of white bread cost 51d. The reason is that the import duties on wheat in Germany amounted to us. 10d. per quarter, whereas in England they are nil. HORSEFLESH AND "HAPPINESS."

We do not suppose that Mr. Smith ever found out during his "short trip" that horses and dogs are slaughtered for food for the poor in Germany. In 1907, 135,239 horses were officially returned as having been killed for food. A United States Consul estimates the number of dogs slaughtered for food in the German Empire in one year at 7,000 - probably more rather than less."

In tbe face of such evidence who dare deny that the German workingmen, their Irives and children, have frequently to live on food which the very poorest of poor people in this country would dis- dain to touch. And how does Mr. Smith reconcile these facts with his sudden discovery of a happiness and prosperiey which " baffle description ''?

MOM

THE BIRTHDAY HONOURS.

MR. F. P. SMITH A BARONET.

The list of the King's birthday honours was issued on Tlureday night, ad coutained at least one announcemebt affording spf cial interest and pleasure to a large number of people in this pait of LaLcashire and Ch.- bbire.

Mr. F. H. Smith of Queen's Lodge' Colwyn Bay, and formerly of Lynnu Hall, Lyrum, has received the signal honour of a baronetcy. Mr. Smith is the son of Mr. Joshua Smith, of Messrs. Joshua Smith and Co., roat,ufacttirers, Oxtord-street, Manchester. 1-4 e was brn St Eccles, and entered his father's business, of which, on its conversion into a limited company' he was made a direct r. He is also chairman of Messrs. Charles Macintosh and Co., Limited, a director of Mes•rs. Broadhurst and Co., Limited, the Garswood Colliery Company, Limited,the Manchester North Borneo Rubber Company, Liniited, the New Eocles Rubber Company, Limited, the New Liverp iol Rubber Company, Limited, and W. Rickard and Co., Derby, and a member of the Ficance Com- mittee of the Bleachers' Association. For many years he has been a leadirg member of the Liberal party in Manchekeer and the district. in West Salford he helped to secure toe returti of the late Mr. Benjamin Arraitag,) in 1885, and at later elections he worked for Mr C E Malet, and took part in the successive victories of Sir George Agnew. In the Eccles division he did much valuable work in support of Mr H J 11 ,by,and afterwards of Sir George Pollard. In the elections of January and December, 1910, I e gave strenuous s,rvice to Sir George Kemp iu his successful fight for Free Trade in North-west Manchester, and v•as treasurer of the Election C mmittee. Mr Smith was attracted to the cause of temperance and social reform when quite a young man, He has been for many years a member of the Executive of the United Kingdom Alliance, and is associated with the Manchester, Salford and District Tempelance Union. He is also one of the hon. secretaries of the Manchester Diocesan Bianch of the Church of England Temperance Soch-ty, and is well known to teinpelance workeis in Warrington as a warm supporter of the Warring,on Baud of Hope Union. He is a justice of the peace for the borough of Salford and the county of Chester. No one in the district has done better work for Liberalism and for all good causes, and as an employer of labour he is greatly respected.

===== 0002601_19120615_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAINING COLLEGE CONCERT.

The concert given by the second year students of the Warringtou Training College, which is always looked forward to with considerable interest, took place on Thursday evening when, notwithstanding the out-door allurements of beautiful summer weather, there was a large attendance in response to the invita- tion of the Principal (Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson). It is usual for the concert to form part of the collective test in the annual examination in music, but on this occasion H.M. Inspector was not present. The principal work selected for performance was Villiers Stanford's " Voyage cf Maeldune," a vivid setting of verses founded on an ancient Irish legend. The choice was an ambitious one, for the work presents exceptional difficulties of interpretation, and it is to the credit of the students that they sang it in a painstaking and expressive way which afforded great pleasure to all who were privileged to be present. Much of the narrative of the weird journey devolves upon the tenor soloist, and Mr. V. Thompson gave this exacting part sympathetic and generally effective treatment. The other principal vocalists,who acquitted themselves admirably, were : Miss Winifred Benty and Miss Jessie Stubbs (sopranos), Miss Margaret Clark and Miss Elsie Wilson (contraltos), and Mr. E. Walker (bass). There was also some choice , concerted singing by the principals and the chorus did their work with precision, the striking contrasts in which the work abounds being finely realized. Efficient service was rendered by an orchestra consisting of the following: —First violin, Mr R H Pearson ; second violin, Mr A Knowles; viola, Mr J Burrows; violoncello, Mr J Jackson; double bass, Mr J Sudworth; pianoforte,Miss Hackett; harmonium, Mr W H Payton, Mus. Bac. Mr W S Woods, who conducted with his usual tact and skill, is to be congratulated upon the gratifying results of the rehearsals which be had so ably directed. The second part of the programme was of a miscellaneous character. It opened with a pianoforte duet " Scherzo and Trio from Symphony N 0.3" (Beethoven) by Misses E Cooke and F Mallatratt whose finished playing fully deserved the hearty recognition which it received. A delightful three-part song " The cloud' (Fletcher), was sung with great taste, and another much-appreciated feature was German's characteristic four-part song "The three knights," in that breezy old pnglish style of which the composer is such a master. Mr R H Pearson gave artistic renderings of the violin solos "Melodie (Henley) and " Mazurka " (IV ieniawaki), and Miss CI Cundv sang " Hark ! hark! the lark" and "Cradle song" (Schubert), in a pleasing and unaffected manner which called forth warm applause.

OPEN-AIR MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

The fourth annual open-air musical festival pro tnoted by the Workingmen's Infirmary Committee,will be held in Bank Park thy kind permission iof his Worship the Mayor and Corporation), on Sunday, the 23rd June, at 3.15 pm. The Warrington Male Choral Union (conductor, Mr. S. Hassall), with Crosfield's band (c inductor, Mr. W Halliwell), will render a choice selection of music. As both these talented organizations are celebrated prize-winners, a rare musical treat will be provided. The Festival will commence with the hymn "Nearer my God to Thee." The audience will be invited to join in the singing of this hymn. Mr. R. Hutton, Messrs. Peter Walker and Sons, Ltd., has kindly promised to provide and erect the large stand for the accommodation of the Band and Choir.

The Workingmen's Committee, in thanking the public for their liberal support to the previc- festivals, look forward witl! confidence to a large attendance and generous financial assistance to the forthcoming Open-air Musical Festival.

A PRESCRIPTION THAT IS ABSOLUTELY FREE.

SOUNDS STRANGE BUT ITS TRUE.

How interesting are the conversations one often hears in public conveyances. The following discus- sion is related as betwera two workmen in a tramcar in a Midland town I see Henry's back to work." "That can't be, as I went to see him only on Satur- day last, and his pains were dreadful. The doctor says he's got Sciatica or Neuritis or something like that, in its worst form, and may be in bed for months."

"Then the doctor is wrong, as I saw him as I came from the works, and he told me he never felt better." "Seeing is believing, but I can't believe it till I see him What did he do?

_ . "He told me that it was a free prescription that he cut from a paper, and as my missus is bad with Rheumatism I asked him for it. Here it is: Go to the nearest chemist's shop and get 60 grains of Kephaldol in tablet form. Take two tablets at once, and then one every hour till pain is gone. He says that's what cured him, and if it don't cure the missus he'll stand me a drink on Saturday night."

WARRINGTON BLUE COAT CHARITY SCHOOL NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the Trustees of the above School will, at their next Quarterly Meeting, on the 15th day of July next, proceed to elect Seven Children (3 Boys and 4 Girls) to Vacancies now exist- ing in the Institution. No candidate will he eligible unless an application on his or her behalf (forms of which may be obtained from the Clerk, at his Office, 3, Winwick Street). shall have been made to the Clerk to the Trustees, at least fourteen days previous to the day of meeting. In electing Children, the Trustees make Orphans their first choice. Those deprived of one parent come next in consideration, and after them the children of parents who have the greatest number and the slenderest means. Dated this 15th day of June, 1910 HENRY ORP:ENALL. Clerk to the Trustees.

BOTELER GRAMMAR SCHOOL, WARRING TON.—An examination will be held at the Grammar School on Saturday, June 22nd at 9 a.m. for toe purpose of electing at least two Boteler scholars. The scholarships cover free tuition and books. Candi- dates are requested to send their names to the head- master not later than Wednesday, June 19th, and must have been for the last three years in attendance at one of the elementary schools in the County Borough of Warrington. Preference will be given to candidates under the age of 12 years on the Ist August 1912 at the discretion of the Governors. The subjects of examination will be :—Aritbmetic, dictation, English grammar, history and geography. A letter testimonial from the candidate's headmaster must be produced. For the information of candidates the election will take place at the meeting of the Governors to be held on the 24th July next. VERE B. DAVIES, Law Clerk.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. ELECTRICITY DEPARTMENT. The ELECTRICITY and TRAMWAYS COMMITTEE INVITE TENDERS for the SUPPLY of 9,500 tons of SLACK to be delivered at the Electricity Works, Howley, during 11 months, commencing Ist August, to be delivered in accordance with conditions of Specification, copy of which can be obtained from the undersigned on payment of One Guinea, which will be returned on receipt of a bona-fide tender. Five shillings will be charged for extra copies of specification. In the alternative the Committee invite fenders for 4,750 tons of SLACK for delivery during the six months commencing Ist August. The Committee also invite Tenders for delivery of the above quantities of Slack by Contract without the usual Strike Clause. The above quantities may at the option of the Engineer be increased during breakdown or other emergency by 10 per cent, above the average supply per day. proved to be Weston's last score and Shaw had a five point win to record. The Bull's Head men will be the hosts to-day and a good game is expected. All the other return matches will be decided in all probability to-day. -- CONSOLATION COMPETITION. SENIOR CONSERVATIVES V EENEETII TANNERY. The Conservatives suffered a shocking defeat at the hands of the villagers at Penketh who had a 73 point victory to their credit and will no doubt fight hard in the return match. There were only two of the Sankey- street men successful in winning and these were ii 1 Anderton who disposed of G Bowyer by 21-11 and T Horrahin who beat E Ashton by 21-19. Straw put up a good fight with W Shave (Penketh) and only lost bythe odd cualk. lerton was the better man all through his game with G Bowyer and led from the first. The players peeled with two each at the fourth end but Ar.derton broke away and maintained the lead until the 25th end when he won by 10 points. -- J Buxton was somewhat off colour in his game with H Glover and only had eight points to his credit to his opponent's 21. T Cooper did worse in his encounter with H Bowyer who had scored his 21 to Cooper's six. -- A Griffiths (Conservatives) pulled up wonderfully in the latter stages or his game with Jack Bowyer and at the 24th end he lost by seven points after having been 8, 11 and 10 points in arrears. J Holland (Conserva- tives) was beaten by J Lloyd all along and the final score after 25 ends was 21-15 'n favour of the villager.

A similar win was recorded for J Henshall (Crosfields) who won 15-11 over J Hudson after nineteen ends. At one time Henshall led by 13-4 and 14-5 but in three ends Hudson added six points and ran his score to eleven. - -- J Hands (Crosfields), came up to expectations and gained a fine win of 13 points over .1 Brown. The game only consisted of 12 ends and the soapery man was the best from the beginning. The captain of the Forshaw's team, T Belliss lost to A Jones by four points after a keen game extending over 23 ends. The score was nine each at. the 16th end and at the 19th end Belliss had a lead of ,11-10. This was his last score and Jones got his five in the next four lengths. --- After having led by 12-8 at the 16th end C J Newton (Forshaws) finally lost to F Howard by the odd point.

DRAW FOR THE SECOND ROUND. The draw for the second round took place on Monday evening, and the committee decided, in view of the lengthy programme before them, to play five matches each Saturday during the second stage, and abandon all practice on Saturdays.
